**Two objections** seem to obtain: (1) that from private ministry *schisms* arise, when in one place a few *particulars*, separating from the community, hold their own peculiar exercises of piety.

*Reply*: These are not *schisms*, since they are recessions not from the Church-malignant but from a legitimate divinely-prescribed separation from the impious and infidels. Nor is it a separation from the Church, since those who use private ministry in a case of necessity are the *particular Church of that place*, joined nonetheless with the universal true and Catholic Church spread throughout the whole world.

Nor is anyone promiscuously to be called a doctor, but he who by other orthodox churches is approved, and about whose sound doctrine there is no doubt.

But even if a great scandal arose from this — it is not to be attributed to the *private ministry* itself but to the *perverse opinions of men*. As it is not permitted to do evil that good may come, so also good is not to be intermitted on account of some evil which is feared. In the public ministry of the *Corinthians* also arose *schisms* on account of the perverse judgments of men, which Paul chastises in the *Prior to the Cor.*, nor is it therefore intermitted; why then should this vice of men hurt private ministry?

**(2)** Some say the orthodox, lest they expose themselves to peril of offending the magistrate, ought to *use the ministry of the adversaries* — just as David once in a case of necessity ate of the show-bread, which was lawful only for other priests to eat (1 Sam 21). By this reasoning, many think the divine precept concerning fleeing idolatry admits a *dispensation*; therefore, so as not to offend the magistrate under whose jurisdiction they live, they think they may attend Mass and other *Papist abominations*, provided that in their heart they contradict them.

But *miserably are they deceived*: **only in ceremonial laws does dispensation have place** — such as the David-and-show-bread case regarding *certain cases* was; but *the moral law never admits dispensation*, for it is the *immoveable and perpetual rule of the divine will*. And to the *moral law* pertains also the *flight from idolatry* and from all abominations, which whoever has acknowledged in his heart, from these also externally in conversation he ought to abstain: otherwise his cult will degenerate into *hypocrisy*, which is contrary to God, who requires not only faith of heart but also confession of mouth from the faithful.

*Nor can any peril be greater than to act against the moral commandments of God*, for the avoidance of which the *corporal and external peril* that threatens from men need not be feared: for God can freely deliver those who cling to him with heart from the hands of enemies. Wherefore Christ wills that we *fear not those who can kill the body, but rather Him who can destroy both body and soul in hell* (Luke 12:5).

### Case IX. Whether the pious can with good conscience live and dwell among enemies of true religion, and by reason of his vocation, which he had from God, refuse to change place, than by living with doubting conscience among them

**Respondeo.** For *nothing is to be done in whatsoever kind of things with doubting conscience*, as gathered from Rom 14:23. But since many make for themselves a scruple in conscience where there is no need, some information in this question is required.

To be known therefore it is, that every Christian lives as it were a *twin life* in this world: (1) *the spiritual*, in which he is called a Christian, and consists in faith, hearing the Word, use of sacraments, and similar parts of divine cult. As regards this life, necessary is *separation of the faithful from the infidels*, of orthodox from heretics: for many admonitions of Paul apply — *what fellowship hath righteousness with unrighteousness? and what communion hath light with darkness?* (2 Cor 6:14); *be ye not unequally yoked together with unbelievers* (2 Cor 6:14); *and Rev 18* — *lest ye be partakers of her sins*, etc. (2) *the political life*, according to which men are, who negotiate, dominate, minister, eat, drink, and do similar things, which pertains to human conversation.

**As regards this life** — one cannot always abstain from conversation with enemies of true religion; otherwise one would have to *depart from the world* (1 Cor 5:10). For *cohabitation is not of light in the darkness of the people*, but of all men dispersed through the world, from which God gathers to himself his Church, in which alone He is acknowledged and worshipped, whose members are not found in one certain place, but through the entire world are dispersed. And of that dispersion *Christ rules in his own kingdom* from sea to sea, even *in the midst of enemies*. Nor does he permit that his people always live among the faithful, but many by reason of their vocation are forced to live among enemies of faith — such as merchants, soldiers, students, and the like, and those also who cannot depart from the paternal seats of their goods.

*In all these, political conversation with enemies of truth is not prohibited.* Therefore Paul (1 Cor 7:12–13) does not command spouses of disparate religion to separate, but *to cohabit*, because *the unfaithful man is sanctified by the faithful wife* — and many serve masters alien from true faith, who therefore need not desert their station: as *in the vocation, in which one is called, so let him remain* (1 Cor 7:20). *Jacob* served in the house of Laban the *idolater* twenty years (Gen 31:41); *Joseph* in the house of Potiphar the Egyptian (Gen 39:4); *Moses* in the house of the Ethiopian priest Jethro (Exod 3); *Daniel* also at the court of *Assyrians* (Dan 1); and Christ himself had his home *among the Capernaites* (Matt 4:13), and with the Pharisees also ate. These *examples of Christ's* show what is lawful in this kind of thing.

But since it is most true that *he who touches pitch shall be defiled thereby* (Sir 13:1), and *he who has fellowship with the proud shall put on pride* — certain cautions are needed if we would preserve a *pure conscience* while dwelling among them:

**1.** *If one who has enemies for cohabitation cannot change his own condition of things*, let him make himself simple and of pure orthodox faith. For consortium with the wicked is dangerous, and often draws to the worse part. Therefore one who among people of pure religion can live, should choose this life more, and by this name congratulate himself: for known is the complaint of David in Ps 120:5: *woe is me, that I sojourn in Meshech, that I dwell in the tents of Kedar!*

**2.** *If one, things not permitting otherwise, is forced to dwell among enemies of truth, let him not imitate their conversations for foreign persons*, but though it is lawful to eat and negotiate with them, let him not do so, but only insofar as necessity and humanity in common life demands. So Christ went to the meals of the Pharisees, but not except when invited.

**3.** *Above all it is to be avoided that in any way with the enemies in religion we consent*, neither by words nor by acts approving their errors — but rather contradicting, when opportunity is given. If we cannot get away without offense, and some *collusion* is sought, let us depart rather from them even bodily, than that we consent to that base hypocrisy. So Christ, even while dining with them, reproved the doctrine and morals of the Pharisees (Luke 14). And it belongs to every Christian *always to be prepared to give a reason for his hope which is in him* (1 Pet 3:15).

**4.** *That we not attend their sacred rites, especially the sacraments* which are the highest matters of religion, and by which we abhor those to whom the sacrament is common. In the vicinity therefore, if it can be done, let him consult for himself and his own about sacred rites, or be content with private exercises of piety — of which more in the preceding.

*Nor are godfathers of Papists to be admitted*, nor should their acts in the sponsorship of baptism be sought — beyond the many other causes of which we spoke in another place; and especially on account of the scandal of the weak brother and species of collusion.

**5.** *No one may become an instrument for propagating false religion*: not as an *amanuensis*, not as a *tabellarius* or *notary* in the cause of religion against confessors of the Gospel; nor in any other way let him promote the *impiety of enemies* — rather let him renounce his office and betake himself to another, though not without loss of his fortune, than to sell his soul to the perpetual dispensation of impiety.

By this reasoning therefore is made a *separation and departure from the enemies of truth in a certain manner*, as *Abraham from Chaldea*, and *Lot from the Sodomites*, and *Jacob from Laban*, went out — the sort of case in which without peril of the soul we can no longer live among them. And in another manner, when we without detriment to religion truly among them stand and dwell, only *approving their impiety in no way*.

— ❦ —

## Chapter VII. On Cases of Conscience Concerning Prayer

*De casibus conscientiae circa orationem.*

Since the chief part of religion is *prayer*, before all things solicitous is the mind of the pious about it — for prayer is the *breath of the soul*, by which we lift up our hearts to God and confess him the fountain of all good. Nor is there any part of the divine cult in which superstition and error creep more easily. Wherefore we shall now propose in order those cases which most often torment consciences.

### Case I. What ought to be attended to in prayer, that our petition may be heard by God

**Respondeo.** Here are to be considered the *conditions of prayer* and of the *one who prays*.

The **conditions of prayer** are three.

**1.** *That it be a due prayer* — that is, directed to the sole God, so that from him alone we ask *all good, and every perfect gift* which comes down from the Father of lights (Jas 1:17). For he alone wills to hear the prayers pouring forth from the heart, and knows how to aid; whereas prayers directed to the saints are *undue*, of which further below.

**2.** *That it be not vain and rash* — for useless and illicit things are not to be sought from God. So the mother of the sons of Zebedee (Matt 20:20), from ambition, asked something for her sons in the kingdom of Christ, which she thought earthly, and received a rebuff. The necessity of things is either of *spiritual things* — such as concern eternal salvation: remission of sins, faith, patience, perseverance in the good, and eternal life itself — or of *bodily things* which pertain to the necessity of this common life: goods of body, of soul, of fortune — and are all comprehended under the fourth petition of the Lord's Prayer in the words *daily bread*.

**3.** *That it be seemly* — such as concerns the glory of God's name and the increase of his kingdom; and which consists *not in multitude of words nor in ornament of words and sentences*, in which for the most part as much verbal ornament accedes so much devotion recedes — but is brief and devout, such as Christ commended when he taught us in praying *not to be many-worded, as the heathen* (Matt 6:7). And Sirach: *let not thy prayer be repetitious* (Sir 7:15). *Repeat the verse*: let it be from the heart, penetrate the ears, be devout.

The **conditions of the one praying** are more.

**1.** *Let him pray in the name of Christ* (John 16:23), by whom we have access to the Father with confidence (Eph 3:12) — *whithersoever the redeemed of Christ are to worship, they are commanded to look to the propitiatory* which is Christ.

**2.** *Let him pray in true faith*, and certain hope of hearing, which Christ has promised. For *not omitting sin* — since no man is *just upon earth, who does good and sins not* (Eccl 7:20) — yet especially from *singular sins*, which wound conscience, we should be strangers when praying; from some of which Scripture names, and by these we may judge of the rest: as **violence and cruelty**, of which the Lord speaks (Isa 1:15): *when ye extend your hands, I will avert mine eyes from you: and when ye multiply orations, I will not hear: your hands are full of blood.* **Unmercifulness**, of which Solomon (Prov 21:13): *he that stoppeth his ears at the cry of the poor, he shall cry, and shall not be heard*. **Inveterate hatred** and rancor of soul against the neighbor, of which Paul (1 Tim 2:8): *I will therefore that men pray everywhere, lifting up holy hands, without wrath*. And Christ (Matt 5:23–24): *if thou bring thy gift to the altar, and there rememberest that thy brother hath ought against thee, leave there thy gift before the altar, and go, first be reconciled to thy brother, and then come and offer thy gift*. **Pride**, of which Peter (1 Pet 5:5): *God resisteth the proud, and giveth grace to the humble*. **Distrust**, of which Paul when he wills us to pray *without doubting* (1 Tim 2:8), and James (1:6): *let him ask in faith, nothing wavering*. From these and similar delicts the one praying should be a stranger.

**3.** *Let him prepare himself well for prayer* — for he enters into the sight of God, who does not admit anything unclean. Whence David says (Ps 108:8): *My heart is prepared, O God, my heart is prepared: I will sing and I will speak a Psalm*. This preparation requires four things: (i) that the one about to pray unburden his mind of all carnal cares and cogitations — for this is *to pour out one's heart before the Lord* (Lam 2:19); (ii) that with reverence full of the divinity he lift up his heart to God, as David: *unto thee, O Lord, do I lift up my soul* (Ps 25:1); (iii) that he weigh with himself, and premeditate, what he means to propose to God — that admonition of Sirach (5:1): *be not hasty in thy speech, nor slow with thy soul to bring forth aught before God*; (iv) that before he prays he shake out his life, and pray with penitence, as David *first washed his hands in innocence, before he encircled the altar of the Lord* (Ps 26:6).

For since *in many things we slip all* (Jas 3:2), and no one knows all his delicts, the work is worth the effort before all things to seek *remission of sins* — which, obtained, we may more confidently pour out our necessities into the bosom of God. Wherefore also *contritionem* of heart the Lord promised to his people (Isa 66:2). Hither pertains a humble mind and *broken heart, which God despises not* (Ps 51:19) — the manner of Daniel and Ezra praying for their people, first seeking remission of all their sins (Dan 9:5, Sir 39:6).

**4.** *Let him pray not coldly but fervently* — especially in graver perils, such were Christ's prayers in the garden (Matt 26:39). And the rule of Christians is *fervent in spirit* (Rom 12:11). Which fervor is excited by temperance of victual — wherefore the ancients, that they might be more alert to prayer (especially the public), used to prescribe *fastings*, as by the example of the Israelites (Judg 20:26) and Davidic (Ps 35:13).

**5.** *Let him pray instantly*, or as Paul speaks, *unceasingly* (1 Thess 5:17) — for God does not immediately according to our nod, so that he must be urged and importuned, that by this reasoning even God may be moved to have mercy (as Christ taught in the parable of the judge and widow, Luke 18:1ff.).

**6.** *Let him pray with desire and appetite of the things to be sought*, as Hanna (1 Sam 1) in her prayers said: *I pour forth the desire of my soul before the Lord*. And to those who so pray, sighs — even without utterance — take the place of prayers, which is clear from the example of Moses (Exod 14:15).

**7.** *Let him pray for spiritual things without condition*, since they can never hurt us; but for corporal things with the condition of the divine will, since sometimes they may be obscure to us — therefore to be committed to the will of God, who knows omnimode what profits us. In this genus is the example of David (2 Sam 15:26 following) — *if I have found favor in thine eyes, let him bring me back*.

*In these modes* if prayers are instituted, they are pious and pleasing, as, so to speak, dictated to God from a holy mind.

### Case II. Whether the Papists are rightly called *invocators of saints*

**Respondeo.** Not here simply is the question of the saints even yet alive, whether they can intercede for us before God — nay, whether they ought to. For this is enjoined by the Apostle James: *pray for one another, that ye may be saved* (Jas 5:16). So Paul asks the Ephesians (Eph 6:18); and one from another this kind of intercession before God may be sought, as Paul from the Ephesians. Nor of the saints of the Old Testament, whom Paul in Heb 11 refers as a *catalogue of saints*, whose beatitude is certain, so that also in the Papacy the proverb has been familiar: *many relics are collected, of which today all remembrance is lost*.

Nor of *fabulous saints*, or such as are doubtful whether they lived thus at all — such are Christopher, Lotobicus, whom they call Godfather, Catharina, Georgius — whom the Papists count among the saints. Some are more ancient, whose true or feigned virtues and acts are proposed in the Church, preached and commended, so that if all things are true, the whole of the Church, seeking the truth by works and *strenuously contending for the truth*, may retain unbroken the immortal faith.

The **imitation of the saints** is enjoined (Heb 13:7): *remember them who have the rule over you, who have spoken unto you the Word of God: whose faith follow, considering the end of their conversation*. And (Heb 6:12): *be followers of them, who through faith and patience inherit the promises*.

Since the works of the saints are of three kinds — precepts, prohibited, and heroic — to be known it is that *imitation is to be instituted only according to the first kind*, in which the saints and we alike are set. Not in the second — for the sins of the saints are not for imitation but *for consolation*, as Ambrose said. Nor of the third — for heroic acts are to be imitated not by us but by those who have a peculiar vocation from God to such things. In this manner the *saints departed* are sufficiently honored by us.

But the Papists, not content with this, moreover add to the cult *invocation of saints*, whom in the *Psalter* also they hail as *lord of heaven and earth*, and to whom things are attributed which cannot be adorned except by fable, and in the Papacy such invocation is proposed and to be believed.

*This distinction is contrary to Scripture* (Rom 1:4 and 3:18), where invocation is *proper to God*, since faith must be had of him who is thence to be invoked. It is *rejected by the most learned of the Papists themselves*, Cajetan, L. Vives, and others. It is *Nestorian*, to divide Christ into a mediator only quoad naturam, quoad naturam humanam. It is *Judaical*, servitude not only to men but even not enough to God (Deut 32:34) — *ye shall serve the God of your fathers* (Mal 3:14), *ye shall serve Christ* (Col 3:24), *serving the Lord Jesus Christ* (Rom 12:11).

The Papists — not content with mere invocation, further add the cult of *hyperdulia* of Mary and other cults, which they say — three causes: (1) on account of our indigence, as Alexander of Hales speaks, whom we should imitate in this discipline, so that at least by their merits we may attain to what by ours we cannot; (2) on account of *the glory of the saints themselves*, whom they call ministers and dispensers of all goods, both spiritual and corporal, as *Gabriel Biel* has it (on *Canon of the Mass*, lection 24); (3) on account of the *reverence of God*.

Hence Lombard writes (iv, dist. 45): *the saints intercede for us with God, and by merit — while by their merit they aid us — obtain what we desire.* Therefore we ask, that they intercede for us, that is, that their merits may suffice for us. And the Council of Trent (Sess. 25) decrees that the saints are to be invoked with confidence, humbly fleeing to their aid.

They teach also, of the *justice of God*, an appeal is to be made to the *forum of the mercy of Mary*, as Bernardinus has it in his *Marial*. And thus all confidence due to God alone is transferred to Mary suppliantly in the *Psalter of Mary*.

> *Notable is the deceit which he plainly proposes.* In the first place stands the ancient sinner, turning his face to the Blessed Virgin with this address: *I beg thee, O pious Virgin, defend me now, Mary. From one side, Mary denudingly begins to address Christ her Son, since she is his mother, mercy I entreat, deigning to point out the sinner. From the other side, Christ shows his wounds to the Father in these words: See the wounds, Father, do that which my mother asks; in the highest constituted the heavenly Father concludes: Whatever thou askest, I will grant, Son, I will deny thee nothing.* [See Scripture pertaining to *D. Gerhard*, p. 713 tom. 5.]

They teach that *by the help of the saints*, prayer is better and more happily brought before God, as Bellarmine has it (i, *De sanctorum beatitudine*, c. 10) — they call the saints ministers and *dispensers* of any goods whatsoever, both spiritual and corporal, as *Gabr. Biel* holds (*super canonem Missae*, sect. 4, lect. 3).

To *singular provinces and cities, animals, and orders* also, singular saints are pre-set — whom not only by prayers but also by *oblations, donaries, vows, dedications of temples, and similar acts*, they religiously worship. All these things in the Papacy are taught in use, and are propounded as things to be believed.

> [To the *Germans* Georgius, to the *Spanish* Jacobus, to the *Gauls* Dionysius, to the *Bulgarians* Ludovicus, to the *Milanese* Ambrosius they set forth. To the *Turchi Sylvanus*, glory to the sows and lambs, to *Gauls of geese*, of sheep Vindelinus, of hogs Antony, of the stables of horses Catherine, of shepherds Lucas — *for the pest is prayed to Sebastian*, for the teeth Apollonia, for the eyes Otilia, for the stone Libanius, for epilepsy Valentine.]

But of all sorts they are, which cannot with safe piety and conscience be admitted — for as fixed it is that saints must be held *neither as mediators, nor as intercessors, nor as helpers, nor by whatsoever other name are they to be invoked* — but whosoever does this, is stained with the crime of *idolatry*, to be settled.

**First, it is clear from the mouth of Christ**: *in vain do they worship God with the mandates of men* (Matt 15:9). And the mandates of men are which have no certain Word. And it is confessed by all Papists, that no dogma is to be received in the Church which does not have foundation in Scripture (Matt 15:13): *every plantation, which my heavenly Father hath not planted, shall be rooted up* — says the Savior (Matt 15:13). And by this same foundation the Samaritan invocation was rejected, though of no time prescription nor patristic authority destitute — because it was *destitute of God's Word*.

*God also in the Law willed idolaters to be punished, because they adored what he himself did not prescribe* (Deut 17:3, 4). The Papists confess, no mandate exists nor exemplum in sacred writings that this be commanded. Right writes Georgius Casander (*Preface*). *Occhus*, in his *Enchiridion*, does not permit *worship of saints* — reasons he adds why in Scripture invocation of saints was not prescribed in the OT: because *the people were otherwise prone to idolatry, and the Fathers were still in Limbo and not blessed*, as (Isa 63:16): *Abraham knew us not, and Israel was ignorant of us* — under the Gospel therefore was not commanded, lest gentiles converted think that they were again introduced to the cult of the ancients; and *for old custom* saints, not as *patrons* but as *gods* to be adored, as when the Lycaonians wanted to sacrifice to Paul and Barnabas. Moreover, if the Apostles and Evangelists had taught it, they would surely have prescribed it. But Paul commands the contrary (Eph 6:18, Heb 4:16).

Add, that *the saints themselves this honor detest*, as *Bartholomew* the angel and *John* the Apostle admit (Rev 22:9): *see that thou do it not, worship God*. And they are our *fellow-servants*, to whom that divine honor is not due — partly because *they are ignorant of our prayers and our calamities in every kind*, since *Abraham* and *Jacob* testify (Isa 63:16). And Augustine (*De cura pro mortuis gerenda* c. 15) and Chrysostom (Hom. 4 *De paenitentia*): *Christ can care for our salvation, but the angels and archangels are not able to know our hearts, they cannot even avert temptations, nor turn our souls to constancy.*

Finally, Scripture teaches that *he alone is to be adored*, in whom faith may be had — since *how shall they call on him in whom they have not believed?* (Rom 10:14). Bellarmine confesses this (i, *De sanctorum beatitudine*, c. 20): *he who invokes the saints as saints and friends of God, in what way ought he to believe in them, though not in full trust of salvation*. But *to believe in a saint is not lawful*: *cursed is the man that trusteth in man* (Jer 17:5). *In God alone is our hope and confidence to be placed* (Ps 118:8–9, Ps 46:1). To his mercy alone we should flee in dangers (Heb 4:16, Ps 145:18).

We ought neither give another the trust that is his (Isa 48:11). Vehemently they sin, who give trust to the saints whom they invoke — which in the Papacy is done most manifestly. **Considered by no doubt these reasons, Christians in the primitive Church** — wherefore the Smyrnaeans, when Jews and gentiles refused to hand over the body of the martyr Polycarp to them, lest perchance forsaking the Crucified they should begin to venerate him, replied: *this indeed cannot be, that we should forsake Christ, who is our salvation and for the salvation of all who in the whole world are to be saved, endured the torments of the crucifixion so that his innocent life might be handed over to the impious, and be adored equally with God*, according to *Eusebius*, *H.E.* iv c. 15.

*Let us follow, therefore, this custom, nor divide the honor of God between him and men.* From God alone, who alone can give, and alone hear the groans of our hearts, let us seek what we need. That he cannot be prayed by others, is what *Tertullian* says (*Apologeticum* c. 30): *another I know not (unless I am mad — indeed of a mad Christ do you say). I am one whose right is to petition him, from whom I gain my beloved things*.

### Case III. Whether whatever we wish we may seek from God

**Respondeo.** *All that we ask from God shall be given us*, if we ask in the *legitimate manner*. The legitimate manner of praying we may gather from the mouth of Christ himself (John 15:16): *every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of lights* — writes James (1:17). It is to be sought therefore, whatsoever is good and useful for us — either in this life, or in the other. **Some things useful to the body, others to the soul, some to time, others to eternity.**

**Whence are the good things to be sought of two kinds:** *some temporal and civil*, as riches, honors, health of body, longevity of life, and of this kind — of which Sirach reminds (Eccl 39:33), and are comprehended in the fourth petition. *Some spiritual and eternal*, as purity of the divine Word, God propitious, assistance of the Holy Spirit, remission of sins, zeal for eternal life, and the like.

**All these things Christ commands us to seek from God the Father, and promises hearing to those seeking in his name** (John 16:23): *whatsoever ye shall ask of the Father in my name, he will give you*. And (Matt 18:19): *if two of you shall agree on earth, as touching any thing that they shall ask, it shall be done for them of my Father which is in heaven*. This is a general promise, if it is done in the legitimate manner.

**We may seek in a legitimate manner** — three maxims noteworthy. Distinguished we must therefore be between things which are *truly useful*, and those which are only *seen to be useful* to us. Truly useful are those which please God, and profit us for ourselves and for our neighbor — of which the Word of God is full. *Falsely seen useful* to us are those which are ingrate to God, and hurt neighbor — and even ourselves interdum — which are sought against the will of God. *Which he wills not, that we make ingrate* to him, or by evil use may harm ourselves. So the success of evil actions is not to be sought from God, as the *Baalites* prayed for the success of their idolatry (1 Kgs 18); Miriam, in her own wickedness, opposed (Num 12:14).

Nor are *rash things* to be sought which are outside our vocation, as the mother of the sons of Zebedee sought (Matt 20:20–23). Not indeed are we from *impatience* to seek, even if it seems useful to us, as Elijah prayed *take away my soul, O Lord, for I am no better than my fathers* (1 Kgs 19:4). *Neither are singular and extraordinary gifts of God to be sought, granted to very few* — such is the gift of miracles, of prophecy, of tongues, as was in the Apostles, which pertain even to those who *have not, God through public ministry of his Word bestows*. **When men are thus swept away from this**, I see not how the hearing of such prayers can be expected.

**Ill also in the Papacy are consciences consulted**, when *votaries* not able to be continent are commanded to seek the gift of continence from God. But *not every man is fit to bear this* (Matt 19:11): *not every one receives this word* — but *each hath his proper gift of God, one after this manner, and another after that* (1 Cor 7:7). And we have no mandate to seek this, no exemplum, no promise of hearing — but *those unable to contain* the Apostle commended *marriage* (1 Cor 7:2, 9).

Therefore, in no way can the conscience of those be strengthened who seek the gift of continence from God, in order to preserve continence in a vow, since they have no mandate that this singular gift, granted to very few, they should seek, and thereby they tempt God, in seeking a gift they know not whether it will please God, and thus they pray with a doubting or even reclaiming conscience — whereas we ought from faith to seek things which we know are pleasing to God, and prescribed in his Word, which vote is not. **Third**, they contemn the order established by God, namely *marriage*, which among other things is *instituted for extinguishing the flames of concupiscence*.

These things being so, some indeed are to be sought from God — but the utility of things not from our own judgment or of other men, but from God's Word and prescript, is to be esteemed.

### Case IV. Whether for all men without distinction we ought to pray

**Respondeo.** *Intercessions for the necessities of other men* are required of us, when Christ himself in the formula which he prescribed for his disciples commanded us to pray in the plural number, since he himself prays for the people (John 17), and he commands us to pray for others (Luke 6). So Paul prays for the Thessalonians (1 Thess 1:2) and for the Ephesians (Eph 6:19); and finally the Apostle James commands us to pray for one another (Jas 5:16).

And this intercession is in general enjoined on us for *all men* (1 Tim 2:1), where some do not understand *the universality of individuals* but only *of kinds* — since they think we should pray *only for the predestined*, as seen at Beza and Molina (2, res. col. Montpel. p. 195). But that gloss cannot have place, since Paul wills us to pray *for every magistrate* even a tyrant, such as was then Nero at the time. Christ also commands us to *pray for our enemies* (Luke 6:28), as he himself did on the cross (Luke 23:34) — for *charity acknowledges no personal exception*.

**There are, however, certain persons of whom Scripture makes mention, for whom by no fixed reason we are to pray.** *Three kinds*:

**1. Not for ourselves and our sons impiously**, so that God may indulge their sins, and grant them happy successes — for this were to pray against God. And in this sense Christ says (John 17:9): *I pray not for the world* — that is, he did not pray by that prayer of Christ, for those, for whom nevertheless he did lay down his precious blood, that in faith they might abide, that they might continue, nor decline — as *Luther* interprets (Tom. 6 *Jena* German, fol. 218 & seq.).

**2. Not for the impious**, so that they may not be converted — if they do not cease their pertinacity, and if at least by evil to their body they cannot be corrected, so that by their example other Israelites may live among them. In this sense the Lord commands *pray for Babylon* (Jer 29:7), which nevertheless afterward by Medes and Persians he brought to inconvenience upon the Jews, when they persevered in their obstinacy which is due to obstinate and impenitent sinners, who admit no correction, and of whom there is no hope of conversion. For the prayers of the pious can even *avert* the punishment of the impious interdum, as *Aaron with his censer averted the plague from the people* (Num 16:46). But when they are in the extreme malice, and are not amended by benevolence of the pious, but rather turn into rage and become worse — one is not to pray any longer for them, that God may spare them of his stripes, lest by trust of that intercession they be hardened in their wickednesses, nor the glory of God through their malice be further obscured, nor by our untimely prayers we resist the will of God, who requires *faith and amendment* of those for whom prayer is made. This once for all God forbade Jeremiah to pray for the obstinate people (Jer 7:16): *pray not for this people, do not lift up cry and prayer for them, and intercede not with me: for I will not hear thee* (Jer 11:14): *pray not for this people, do not lift up cry and prayer for them, for I will not hear them in the time of their affliction* (Jer 14:11–12): *though they fast, I will not hear their cry, and though they offer burnt offering and oblation, I will not accept them*.

**3. Not for those who *sin unto death*.** This is expressly prohibited (1 John 5:16): *there is a sin unto death: I do not say that he shall pray for it* — which passage as it is difficult, so has been variously expounded.

*Some thought*, prayer is not to be made for those *who have fallen into mortal sin from the Church* — as they hold Cyprian (Ep. 26 and 55), *Ambrose* (i, *De poenitentia* c. 7), *Augustine* (*Ep. Parmenionen*), *Gregory* (vi, *Moral.* c. 33), whom Elias Cretensis follows (tom. 1, in Nazianzen), and *Rupertus* in *Moralium* ix, c. 11, fol. 788 — which sentence is indeed true; but John speaks not of prayers for the dead but for the living.

*Prosper thinks* the Apostle John wishes, *lest imperfect men* — such as those to whom he was then writing, and who were even still polluted with sins — should pray for graver delicts of others, when they themselves are heard for the lighter, since for graver the intercession of *the just* (such as Moses, Jeremiah, and similar men) is to be reserved.

But that distinction is not acknowledged in the text of John — but, as *Chrysostom* would have it, if all without distinction pray for the brother not sinning unto death, so all forbidden it is to pray for him who sins unto death. Nor indeed does God hear prayers for those unfit for repentance and singular in impiety — because *he cannot be judged from the mouth of infants* to be praised in some way (Ps 8:2). *Jerome* (1 c. *Iovinianum*) esteems John teaches that indeed for a sin unto death one is difficultly heard, which interpretation Peter Lombard (iv, sent. dist. 15, q. 1), *Bellarmine* (i, *De poenitentia* c. 16) and *Serarius* (*Commentar.* thereto) follow. But the Apostle's words have nothing of the difficulty of hearing — but he clearly prohibits prayers.

*To be known* therefore is that *St John speaks not of any mortal sin, but plainly and singularly of the sin* which is called *malicious against the Holy Spirit* — because sin unto death is called, since such a sinner, when *he contumaciously tramples underfoot the medicine of his conversion*, that is, the meritum of Christ, is expected only to see eternal death, whence also it is called *irremissible* (Matt 12:32, Mark 3:29, Luke 12:10).

*The requisites* of such sin, from which it can be recognized, are these: **1.** *Denial of the Gospel with blasphemy* (Matt 12:31); **2.** the true and evident recognition of *heavenly doctrine* (Heb 6:4 and 10:16); **3.** *voluntary defection* (Heb 10:29 and 6:6); **4.** *rejection of the mediation of Christ* — which conversion Scripture calls making the Holy Spirit alien (Heb 10:29). When therefore of such a sinner it is settled, that he is of this ilk — since prayers *would be poured out without fruit*, in that same manner the prohibition of the Spirit is joined to his prayer.

But when it happens that *someone hidden yet knows himself to have sinned unto death*, we can pray for him with good conscience — that the Lord, if he unknowingly denied and opposed the truth, deign to have mercy on him, and lead him back into the way, as *Luther* teaches more fully, since God wills that all wandering men should be reduced into the way, as *Augustine* also (*Sermo* on John, tract. 43, c. 12) has written: *of which vessels of election, since we cannot as long as they in this life live constituted, they are neither reprobate nor sperandi, nor as pertaining to us to pray for them incongruously with piety, that they may become more merciful*.

— ❦ —

### Case V. Whether it is lawful to pray for the departed

**Respondeo.** From *prayer for the dead*, the Romanists prove their *Purgatory* — one fable by another. But of those prayers, such as in the Papacy are poured out for the dead, nothing is written in Scripture. **Nor is one to pray for the departed in any manner, as if they had need of our intercession.** For if they have piously departed, they now rest in the *quiet* of eternity (Rev 14:13), nor do they suffer torments (2 Sam 3:8). What then avail our prayers? But if they have died in their sins without repentance, their habitation is in *the eternal hell, from which there is no redemption*. *In hell there is no redemption*, says *Augustine* (*De peccatorum meritis et remissione* c. 34), *nor can this happen unless with the devil, who is the head of them, is fallen* — CHRIST himself. *Well, therefore, replies that pupil to Damascene, since he remembers, of Damascene's book of temporal reference, 105*: *when the dead one of his parents makes memory, or is even at the sacred offering, or if is set forth of the dead constant blandishments and promises, so that they may be freed from Purgatory, and go, that many things after death of monks Christ himself prescribed, nor the Apostles commended.*

One only passage there is, in the apocryphal book (2 Macc 12:43ff.), where *the Maccabees* for their departed are read to have offered — for those namely, who in battle had taken something of the *donaries* (v. 40). But that prayer was only a *vow*, that sin might no more be imputed at the resurrection, and lest by reason of the delict admitted, the rest might be utterly forsaken. Nor can we deny that the *Maccabees* imitated many gentile-manners and rites of the gentiles closely — to which emulation here also may perhaps refer.

From the gentiles indeed this rite had its origin, who over the dead sacred rites feigned, or with libations of the sepulchres of these, they wet — as if for the *pathway of return* they sacrificed to the infernal gods for their souls, whom they held sacred, so that they might be placated toward them, lest the returning souls of their fathers put obstacles for the passageway or fright them. So *Virgil* in *Aeneid* v (v. 76): *the goblets he libated to father Anchises*, and so on. This gentile custom later Christians retained, so that they might not seem inhuman to their dead: at the *paternalia* of friends they offered something, but for use of the poor, not for placating God. They added prayers also over the dead, of whom the pious were certain, that they had come *to the terminus of their contests* — not for the reason that *the memorial of the just is in joy and immortal hope*, as *Wisdom* speaks (Wis 4:16), Ecclesiastes (Sir 7:5).

And these prayers concerned bodies given to earth, not souls stationed in Purgatory — as it has been in the *Liturgy of Basil* to have: *remember, O Lord, of all those who are dead in the hope of resurrection of eternal life* — of the dormant he speaks *in hope of resurrection*, which is of the bodies not of the souls. By these prayers they wished not to merit for them the grace of GOD, or the eternal life — but partly to congratulate the departed, partly to instruct the survivors, that they might contend for a blessed death.

So Origen writes (*In Iob*): *in faith of the departed devoutly we make memory, so that indeed of these to be resurrected in joy, we also may seek a pious consummation to be gained in faith*.

*In this sense the Apology of the Augsburg Confession* (Art. 11 & 24 *De vocabulo Missae*) writes: we do not prohibit prayer for the dead — not understanding *oblation for the departed*, by which they are freed from the place of torments, such as the patrons of Purgatory in the Papacy understand — but by the style of the ancient Church, *vows*, by which we wish the departed to be well, well hoping for their beatitude, and exhorting us to a similar exit.

*Prayers* for the souls in *Purgatory* about the time of *Gregory the Great*, from superstitious apparitions of spirits or *spectra* rather, arose about the year of Christ *600* — since we know, *truth is not to be sought from the dead* (Deut 18:11), we leave that superstition to its author, together with annexed quaestiunculae — whether *suffragia* for many at once made be as efficacious as for one; whether *for one alone* made benefit all in Purgatory; whether *suffragia* by those who are in mortal sin, when made for the departed profit them? — of which see *Lombard* (iv, dist. 45), and *Mornaeus* (*De sacra Eucharistia* iv, c. 11, sub fine).

**We in our Savior have faith** (John 5:24): *he that heareth my word and believeth on him that sent me, hath everlasting life, and shall not come into judgment, but is passed from death unto life*. That this may happen to all of us, let us pray for one another *while yet we are surviving* — to the dead the *suffrages of the living profit nothing*, unless perhaps you would believe *what Durandus writes* concerning the efficacy of prayers for the dead (in *Sent.* iv, dist. 25, q. 2), from *life* — namely, the damned were not otherwise helped by the prayers of Macarius, except that they might see one another in torments. And of this they could not have gladness, unless of a false and phantasmal kind, such as is common to the wretched, to *see companions in evils*, according to that verse: *comfort of the miserable is to have companions of evils*; just as *demons rejoice*, they say, when men are drawn to sins, yet the punishment inflicted on damned men is *not lessened* by that of the demons.

Egregious indeed the consolation which the *patrons of Purgatory* hold for themselves; ours, that in the *bosom of Abraham* is the eternal consolation. We know that those who *have piously fallen asleep in the Lord* feel no torment with the rich man, who *dies in his sins*. Of them, *by our prayers is no work needed* — as of themselves, no rest thence can be hoped for.

### Case VI. Whether saints can daily seek remission of sins with good conscience

**Respondeo.** So indeed in expressed words David teaches (Ps 32:6): *for this cause, namely, remission of sins, shall every one that is godly pray unto thee in a time when thou mayest be found*. But the cause of doubting arises from the doctrine of our Church concerning the *certitude of grace*. For if we are already *certain with the certitude of faith* — which admits no falsity — that sins have been forgiven us, what need is there of praying *for remission of sins*? By which ram Bellarmine attacks the certitude of grace (i, *De iustificatione* c. 10): *if I am to believe certainly with divine faith*, he says, *that my sins have been forgiven me by Christ, how can I say with the publican*, "God be merciful to me a sinner", or with the Apostles and the universal Church, "forgive us our debts" — unless disturbing faith, I would make God a liar?

But this is a *cavil* that easily is dissolved. For though of past sins we may be certain, that they are forgiven, if we have sought their remission in true faith — yet since we have many *hidden delicts* (Ps 19:12), and in our works even just some *uncleanness* clings (Eccl 7:20), we daily also are prone to sins — therefore always are we to pray, that God pass over those sins for us: which saints have often done, who never doubted of the remission of past sins.

Moreover, it is not absurd to *seek from God the continuation of that good* which we already believe we have obtained. For we also pray, *that the kingdom of God may come to us, that the will of God may be done*, which nevertheless we do not doubt is already made — but *that we may continually enjoy this good*, always must be prayed. For *faith and prayer* are conjoined; and what GOD wills us to believe, that also for the future he commands us to seek.

### Case VII. On the certainty of our being heard in prayer

**Respondeo.** The Papists deny, that anyone with infallible certainty can be certain, that God hears his prayers, unless it is exactly known to him that he has faith and is in grace: and that no man knows this without a special revelation. Yet they attribute to man a certain *obscure certitude*, which Bellarmine calls *conjectural* (i, *De iustificatione* c. 12), and attribute it to those who rely on so many signs and conjectures that they render man secure and exclude anxiety, but do not expel all *fear*. Such certitude he concedes to those who pray (c. 9), where he writes: *that the Holy Spirit which moves us to say "Abba Father" is not known to us by divine faith, but only by conjecture, which can be fallacious*. See the *Fathers of the Council of Trent* (Sess. 6, Can. 9 and 13).

But that certitude which rests on fallacious conjectures is really *none*, and produces mere doubt and anxiety of soul. Wherefore we do not err, if we say that the Papists remove all certainty from prayers, whence they call it *presumption and temerity* if anyone should promise himself the most certain hearing of his prayers.

**Against this error we set up the soul of man.** We settle that *prayers rightly formed are not vain*, but *are certainly heard* — if not according to *our will*, yet according to *our utility*. Prayers are rightly formed when we invoke God, and found our prayers on the *mandate and promise* of God, and pour them forth from a heart truly penitent in true faith.

**That such prayers are heard:** (1) *The ratio of faith itself teaches, which is the soul of prayer*. For true faith is an immovable and firm confidence of soul, apprehending the promises without doubt — therefore doubt is said to be *of diffidence*, and opposed to faith (Rom 4:20). (2) *We have express promise of God in the Word*: *call upon me, and I will deliver thee* (Ps 50:15); *before they call, I will answer* (Isa 65:24); *if two of you shall agree on earth as touching any thing that they shall ask, it shall be done for them of my Father which is in heaven* (Matt 18:19); *ask and doubt not* (Luke 11:9); *whatsoever we ask according to his will, he heareth us* (1 John 5:14). By these words of God we rightly have faith: *for it is impossible that God should lie* (Heb 6:18). (3) *These promises are moreover confirmed by an oath*: *verily, verily, I say unto you, whatsoever ye shall ask the Father in my name, doubt not* (John 16:23), which oath was added for this cause, that the *firmness and immobility of the divine counsel* be shown, and that *we might have strong consolation* (Heb 6:16–17). For *the oath of God is the confirmation of the promise and reproof of unfaithfulness*, as *Augustine* says (16, *De civ. Dei* c. 32): *O blessed us, for whose sake God swears! O most wretched, if we do not believe God even when he swears!* As *Tertullian* has it (*De poenitentia*): *for a triple cause Christ was accustomed to swear*. (i) To excite attention (John 8). (ii) For the increase of the unbelieving (Matt 8): *what is the swearing of the true God, but the increase of the unbelieving?* — as Tertullian says. (iii) To exclude doubt. So Augustine: *God's oath be our confirmation* (compare 2 Sam 13:29, John 8:16, Heb 6:12, Rom 5:1, Ps 51:6, Ps 109:1, 4). (4) *Many inconveniences follow doubting*: it makes prayers vain — *for he that asketh in faith* (says James, 1:6) *nothing wavering, he that wavereth is like a wave of the sea driven with the wind and tossed: for let not that man think that he shall receive any thing of the Lord* (Jas 1:6). It *accuses God of lying* — for *he that believeth not God hath made him a liar* (1 John 5:10). It takes away solid consolation in adversities: for *prayers and tears* are the *arms of the Church, when we know not what to do, only this we have, that our eyes we direct to God* (2 Chr 20:12).

If there is no certitude of hearing, our *very best consolation is snatched away*, the door is opened to *desperation*, and the testimony of the Holy Spirit is enervated. For he testifies that we are *sons of God* (Rom 8:16); it is of *sons* to have confidence in their *parents*, that from them they will obtain whatsoever with filial confidence they shall ask (Christ testifies, Luke 11:11–13, Matt 7:9–11).

**Nevertheless always let the discrimination of the things to be sought be observed**, which above we touched. If the goods are *spiritual and eternal*, they are never denied to those who rightly pray, since it is always expedient that they be given us — therefore *without condition of the divine will* we ask for them, for that God wills to give them us his Word makes plentifully clear. But if the goods are *temporal*, pertaining to this present life, they are to be sought *with the condition of the divine will*: and these prayers God always hears, though not always according to our pleasure, yet according to our benefit.

**Sometimes he gives at once, what we ask** — as *health to the leper* (Matt 8). **Sometimes he defers assistance**, and in the meantime increases consolation — as *David's prayers*, which he poured out in exile, were at last heard. **Sometimes he denies outright** what we seek, because he saw it did not profit us, and he gives *something better* — as to Paul who instantly begged the *removal of the thorn in his flesh*, was answered: *my grace is sufficient for thee, for my strength is made perfect in weakness* (2 Cor 12:9). *What is more useful*, a body in health, or a body afflicted? — asks *Prosper*.

*Acquiescence therefore is always in the will of God* — nor does it fail the pious hearing, when he does not immediately succor us in trouble according to our nod. For both *denial of harmful things* and *supplying of consolation* or of a *better good* are a species of hearing. So *Augustine* writes (*In John*, tract. 4): *sometimes God is said not to hear our prayers, when he does not do what we ask — which happens when we ask things which we think will be useful, and in reality are not, but rather harmful*. See more in Commentary on 2 Corinthians ch. 12, pt. 1, art. 4.

### Case VIII. Whether it is even lawful to pray *against* others, and to imprecate evils on them

**Respondeo.** *Augustine* (i, *De sermone Domini in monte*) sets forth this as doubtful: *whether just as for some we are not to pray*, so *against some also we are to pray*, is not sufficiently apparent. And indeed too frequent are curses and imprecations, by which one man ill-speaks against another — often against the innocent, by which reasoning he hurts himself more than others. For from such prayers *maledictions* arise, by which what is *divinely ordained for our salvation*, others seek should turn to their perdition.

In genus therefore to be known it is, *from all rash malediction abstinence is to be made*. Those who are attacked interdum by imprecations, who either lightly have offended another, are prayed against, that GOD may destroy them, strike them with lightning, that earth swallow them and the like — such prayer is *from choler*, and betrays *impotence of soul*, and overturns all *charity of neighbor*. Let there here have place that of Wisdom (Wis 1:11): *keep yourselves therefore from murmuring, which profiteth nothing, and refrain your tongue from backbiting; for there is no word so secret that shall go for naught; and the mouth that lieth slayeth the soul* — for men, who *are made in the likeness of God* (Jas 3:9).

Moreover, even if we had *just cause* to imprecate against others, yet we are not easily to do it — but let us first consider both ourselves and those against whom we wish to imprecate.

We are either **private persons**, or **public persons**, such as parents, teachers, pastors, magistrates, and whomever else divinely are pre-set to others.

*To private persons* it is said (Matt 5:44): *love your enemies, bless them that curse you, do good to them that hate you, and pray for them that despitefully use you and persecute you*. So (Rom 12:14, 17, 21): *bless, and curse not; render to no man evil for evil; be not overcome of evil, but overcome evil with good*.

**These therefore who pray against enemies either private or public of God and the Church, not from *hatred against a person* (this would be against love, which is owed even to enemies) but from *love of GOD* and from *just zeal* against vices — impediments of God's honor and *of common tranquillity*.** So *Paul* also wished to pray for the Thessalonians against his enemies importunate and *evil men impeding the course of the Gospel* (2 Thess 3:1–2).

*Public persons* pray: (1) against *public enemies*, as David against the enemies of the Church (Ps 6:10): *let all mine enemies be ashamed and sore vexed; let them return and be ashamed suddenly*. (Ps 35:1–2): *plead my cause, O Lord, with them that strive with me, fight against them that fight against me. Take hold of shield and buckler, and stand up for mine help*, etc. Similar things see (Ps 69:29): *let them be blotted out of the book of life, and not be written with the righteous*. (Ps 94:2): *lift up thyself, thou judge of the earth: render a reward to the proud*, etc. So Paul prays *against Pseudo-Apostles disturbers of the Church*: *I would they were even cut off, which trouble you* (Gal 5:12). And against Alexander the coppersmith: *Alexander the coppersmith did me much evil: the Lord reward him according to his works* (2 Tim 4:14).

(2) They pray *against those who are subject to them*, when contumaciously and in evil counsel they resist — as *Noah* imprecated against his incorrigible son *Ham* and his posterity (Gen 9:25); and many parents against unruly children *groan*. So sometimes contumacious *hearers*, contemners of the divine Word, are threatened — to which is referred the imprecation of the Apostle Paul (1 Cor 16:22): *if any man love not the Lord Jesus Christ, let him be anathema, maranatha*.

It appears from these, that it is *sometimes lawful to pray against others*: but lest in this kind we should sin against *Christian love*, the following cautions are to be observed.

**1.** *Let prayers not be made promiscuously against just anyone*, whether he be our private or public enemy of God and the Church — but only against those who are *incorrigible*. For while there is yet hope of *amendment*, let prayers rather be made for their *conversion*. This is how *Christ prayed for those crucifying him*: *Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do* (Luke 23:34); and *Stephen* for those stoning him (Acts 7:60). Which prayers were not in vain. For Christ by his prayers won the *Centurion and many Jews under the cross*; and Stephen won Paul. So *Christ commands us to pray for our enemies* (Matt 5:44); Paul in general *for all men*, even *tyrant magistrate* such as Nero was (1 Tim 2:1); and Jeremiah enjoins the Jews to *pray for the city* in which they were held captive (Jer 29:7): *seek the prosperity of that city* (he says) *whither I have caused you to be carried away captives, and pray to Jehovah for it*.

**2.** *Against pertinacious and quite incurable enemies indeed pray — but let the prayers be directed not so much against the person, as against the cause* — namely that God resist their attempts, impede their pernicious counsels, keep them occupied with other business, so that they may not disturb our tranquillity and that of the Church. In this manner *David prayed against Ahithophel* (2 Sam 15:31): *infatuate, O Lord, the counsel of Ahithophel*. And the Apostles prayed against the *Pontifs and Pharisees* of the Jerusalem council (Acts 4:29): *now, O Lord, behold their threats, and grant unto thy servants, that with all boldness they may speak thy Word*.

**3.** *Against the *persons* of the enemies no one imprecates, unless from revelation of God he is certain that the man against whom he wishes to pray is incurable*, and therefore already by divine judgment destined to punishment — which *David* and *Paul* could know for certain, and therefore their imprecations were rather *Prophecies* of what would befall the impious, than prayers to make happen. As when David in Ps 109 prays against the people of the Jews, especially against Judas the traitor: *when he shall be judged, let him be condemned: and let his prayer become sin. Let his days be few; and let another take his office. Let his children be fatherless, and his wife a widow*, etc. And Ps 59:6: *have thou no mercy on those who work iniquity: they shall return at evening: they shall suffer hunger like a dog, and shall run about the city*.

Of these and similar sharper words of the *holy men* writes *Augustine* (16, *Contra Faustum*, and against the *Manichaeans* c. 21): *maledictions are said from Prophecy, and are not from evil vow, but from prediction of the Spirit denouncing. For those which are of evil vow are prohibited, when it is said, "bless, curse not". But those are found sometimes in the discourse of the saints, as of the Apostle Paul: "Alexander the coppersmith did me much evil: the Lord will reward him according to his works"*. Whence it is clear, that the exemplum of David or Paul is not proposed for imitation to anyone, since the *spirit of discretion and of Prophecy* is not given to all.

**4.** *Even those who pray against incurable persons of the enemies of God and pious men, let them not do this from a vicious affect but from zeal commending the justice and power of GOD in overturning his enemies*: therefore let them leave to God the execution of their imprecation, either to the perdition or to the conversion of the impious. For sometimes the *temporal confusion of the impious* serves for the conversion of the impious, according to that of David (Ps 83:17): *fill their faces with shame, that they may seek thy name, O Lord*.

But if *conversion* follows, the prayers of the pious against the impious are *nothing else than approbation of the divine sentence*, already made in Scripture — nor are they referred to the *punishment of man*, but *to the justice of him punishing* — which ought to please all, though the pious should prefer that all should live in such a manner, that the *mercy rather than justice* of GOD found place in them. Of which David says (Ps 58:11): *the righteous shall rejoice when he seeth the vengeance*.

**These things considered**, *prayers against the impious* can be instituted in a *legitimate manner and mode*.

### Case IX. Whether during prayer external gestures may be admitted

**Respondeo.** As the kingdom of God comes *not with observation* (Luke 17:20), so *God, to whom prayer is directed, leaves all created things far behind*. So *Augustine* writes (i, *De sermone Domini in monte* c. 5): *when we stand to prayer, we turn to the east, whence heaven rises — not as though God were there, as if he had deserted the other parts of the world, who is everywhere present, not by spaces of place, but by power of majesty; but that the soul may be admonished to convert itself to a more excellent nature, that is to the Lord — since even his body, which is earthly, is converted to a more excellent body, that is to the heavenly body*.

Nor is the true cult of God, by which subjects of his kingdom are affected, consisting in external corporal gestures. And since Scripture prescribes nothing certain in this kind, nor prescribes certain gestures to those praying — therefore neither is conscience bound to anything certain and determined.

In general nonetheless to be known is, that since our prayers are to be made *with devotion and humble spirit*, the voice and every habit of the body ought to be so composed, that it *breathe humility and submission*. For even the *holy angels* standing before the throne of God covered their faces with wings — thereby testifying that they were unworthy to behold the face of God, nor could they bear the majesty and splendor of God (Isa 6:2).

The *ancients while praying turned their face to the east*, as often as they cultivated — as the sun and moon hold a certain principality of their order to God, to whom prayer is directed, all created things to leave behind. *Damascene* (iv, *De orthodoxa fide* c. 12) has many reasons: (1) Christ is called *east* in Scripture; (2) Christ crucified faced west, but assumed was borne to the east; (3) from the east he will come to judgment; (4) toward the east faced the Mosaic tabernacle and the temple of Solomon; (5) toward the east was planted the *garden of Eden* — therefore men by this ceremony seek that ancient country whence the first man was expelled.

**In Scripture various gestures of those praying are expressed** — as regards the chief members and posture of the body. **They prayed with head uncovered**, as clear from that of the Apostle (1 Cor 11:4): *every man praying with head covered, dishonoreth his head* — from which the author of the *De contemtu mundi* (attributed to *Augustine*) c. 4 rebukes clerics, who while singing psalmodies and spiritual hymns wear caps, miters, and other coverings on their heads. **They prayed with lifted eyes to heaven**, as Christ (John 17:1). **They prayed weeping**, as Hanna (1 Sam 1:10). **They prayed on bended knees**, which David daily *seven times a day* by his prayers did (Dan 6:10); and Christ in the garden by this posture prayed (Luke 22:41). Paul also for the Church bent his knees (Eph 3:14). Hither some refer that of Isa 45:23: *unto me every knee shall bow*; and Phil 2:10: *at the name of Jesus every knee should bow*, etc.

**They prayed with hands lifted up to heaven**, as *Solomon* before the ark of the Lord (1 Kgs 8:22); and Paul writes: *I will therefore that men pray in every place, lifting up holy hands* (1 Tim 2:8). **They prayed prostrate on the ground**, as *Christ* (Matt 26:39). They also *prayed standing* — as of the Pharisee and Publican (Luke 18:11–13); and Christ: *when ye stand praying* (Mark 11:25) — which *Augustine* thinks was done *in sign of resurrection* (Ep. 119, c. 14). They *also prayed sitting*, as *Elijah* sitting under a juniper tree prayed to God (1 Kgs 19:4); *David* also sitting prayed (1 Chr 17:16).

**All these are not of necessity, but of liberal observation** — otherwise the gesture and habits of the body would not have varied so, since even the *sick can pray in bed*, as Hezekiah did (2 Kgs 20:1). Nor do we adopt gesture in praying, that our prayers may be more grateful to God — but, as *Augustine* writes (i, *De cura pro mortuis gerenda* c. 5): *those who pray in their bodily members do what suits suppliants: when they bend their knees, when they extend their hands, or even prostrate themselves on the ground, or something visibly do — although their invisible will and heart's intention are known to God, nor does he need this indication so that a human soul be opened to him — but by these gestures man rather excites himself to prayer, and be humbled the more fervently*.

**These gestures of the pious make of prayer *adoration***. So *Augustine* distinguishes these two (i, *Contra Maximum* c. 5): *adoration is done with gesture as a sign of humility, prayer or petition is even without gesture* — in which manner even the *Holy Spirit* is said to *pray and intercede for us* (Rom 8:26), though he does not adore the Father.

**Given that as regards *gestures* certain precepts cannot be given in prayers**, in general however to be held is, that in *public* prayers each one should follow the rite of the particular Church in which he dwells — for *dissension in accustomed rites gives birth to schism*. In *private* prayers, however, let each follow that which the affect of his soul expresses, excites, moves, inflames — which is done by *modest* gesture of the body, *honest and grave*: for always in prayers greater ratio is to be had of the *soul* than of the *body*, since *the eyes of the Lord behold the heart* (1 Sam 16:7).

### Case X. What language ought to be used in prayers to God

**Respondeo.** Nothing certain indeed in this genus can we prescribe: for God is the author of all languages, and looks not so much on the *voice* as on the *heart* of him who prays. It is however to be distinguished between *public* and *private* prayers, and between men skilled in languages and ignorant.

**In private prayers each may use whatever language**, so long as it be known to him: for even idiots and the female sex should not be strictly bound to the Latin language. The Apostle Paul disapproves this (1 Cor 14:14–15): *if I pray in an unknown tongue, my spirit prayeth, but my understanding is unfruitful: what is it then? I will pray with the spirit, and I will pray with the understanding also: I will sing with the spirit, and I will sing with the understanding also*. The Apostle therefore wills, that prayer be in a *language which one understands*, that he may know *what he speaks with God* — for attention and devotion in prayers is required, which in unintelligible prayers cannot be. Therefore the Apostle writes (v. 5): *I would that ye all spake with tongues, but rather that ye prophesied* — where indeed he admits prayer in a *foreign tongue*, but so that we may also understand and dare say what we ask.

**Public prayers** are to be instituted in the language which is *vernacular* to the Church — this is by far more expedient and more consonant to Scripture, for the following reasons:

The *Council of Trent* (Sess. 22, Can. 9) and its chief doctors — Bellarmine (ii, *De verbo Dei* c. 15 and 16), Stapleton (*Antidot.* on 1 Cor 14), Sixtus Senensis (*Bibliotheca* vi, *Annot.* 264) — set up *Latin* as the *most common* tongue for public prayers. Whence *ignorant monks and nuns mumble psalms and prayerlets* in a language they do not understand.

**But we hold that public liturgies and litanies ought to be in the vernacular**, for these reasons:

**1.** *The Apostolic authority of Paul*: 1 Cor 14 whole chapter refers to *edification*. In that same chapter by many arguments he shows, that a foreign tongue in *public sacred things* edifies nothing. It profits indeed the speaker, not however the hearer (v. 2) — nay, the prayers of the Papists do not always profit even the speaker, as the psalmodies of nuns show. *Prayers* and all *public offices* ought to be done *for the consolation and exhortation of the Church* — which end unknown tongues do not attain (v. 3); an unknown tongue neither prepares for prayer, nor moves affections, just as a trumpet giving an uncertain sound prepares no one for battle (v. 8); *a tongue without sense is a sound without mind* (v. 10); to an *unknown language an idiot cannot say Amen* (v. 16), because he does not understand what has been said (v. 16); he who uses in prayers a language *unknown to others*, gives thanks well indeed, but the other is not *edified* (v. 17). *An unknown tongue pertains to the unbelieving, who are not worthy to understand*, notes *Ambrose* on v. 22. Those saying sacred things in an unknown tongue are *seen to be insane* to the hearers (v. 23).

This concentration of Pauline arguments moved *Cajetan* — as he writes in his commentary on that chapter — to admit: *from this doctrine it is held, that better for the edification of the Church it is, that prayers, which are said with the people listening, be spoken in the common language for clerics and the people, than in Latin — Bellarmine's exceptions in the commentary of mine on the first Epistle to the Corinthians I have examined. See part 1, quest. 1, p. 776*. This first ratio therefore stands firm and immobile, since it is *Apostolic*.

**2.** *The minister in public prayers is the mouth of the Church*, in whose name he speaks to God, just as before he is the mouth of God, in whose name he speaks to the people. As therefore a foreign tongue is not to be used for *teaching*, so neither for *praying*: for *no one is edified by hearing what he does not understand*. And *Augustine* writes (ii, *De Genesi ad literam* c. 5): *by those tongues which we do not understand, we are deaf*, says *Cicero* (*Tuscul. quaest.* v). But public prayers ought to be made *with attention and devotion of the people*, which in foreign tongues cannot be, since the voice unintelligible touches at most the ears, but affects not the heart.

[*Bellarmine excepts*: (i) *only in spiritual exhortations is a foreign tongue prohibited*. **Reply**: Paul speaks of *divine cult in its exercises*, in which the *people respond Amen* — which is not in exhortations.
(ii) *Paul speaks of canticles which the Christians composed for praising God*. **Reply**: this is not so — but in public assemblies were used the *Davidic Psalms* (Eph 5:19, Col 3:16).
(iii) *Ministers do not pray to the people but to God for the people. Therefore it is not necessary that the people understand*. **Reply**: thus perhaps it would be better, if the Papists *left the people at home*, if they need not understand what is being done in the temple.]

**3.** *Public prayers offer consolation to the afflicted*, who often for prayer's sake come to public assemblies: but without understanding they have no consolation, but rather terror — for *tongues serve as a sign to unbelievers*, says the Apostle (1 Cor 14:22). A *sign* indeed terrifies, but does not teach, nor helps at all — nay often harms, as *Oecumenius* and *Theophylact* say on this passage.

**4.** *In the Old Testament, the psalms and public prayers were sung in the vulgar tongue*. Concerning the psalms this is clear from David giving his singers *4,000 psalms to sing* — learned and unlearned alike (1 Chr 25:8); and at the *dedication of the temple* all the sons of Israel *adored and praised God saying* from Ps 106:1: *give thanks unto the Lord, for he is good, for his mercy endureth forever* (2 Chr 7:1, and 1 Ezra 3:11); also at the *entry of Christ to Jerusalem* the verses 15 and 16 of Ps 118 were sung by the multitudes going before and following, and by the *little children* (Matt 21:9, 15).

Concerning other prayers, that they were said in a language known to all, we gather from the *history of Jehoshaphat* (2 Chr 20:5ff.) — where the pious king prayed against the *Ammonites and Moabites*, with the Israelites present along with little ones, wives and children — whose presence would have been futile, if they had not understood those prayers. Therefore they too *fell prone on the earth and adored* (v. 18); nay, together with the Levites *with harmonious voice they said*: *confess ye unto the Lord, for his mercy endureth forever* (v. 21).

**5.** *Also in the primitive Church of the New Testament it was accustomed*, that prayers with other sacred things were done *in the language known to all*. *Justin Martyr* (*Apol.* 2, near the end) refers: *how they sanctified the day of the Sun, on which all who lived in the towns and countryside came together into one place; how they read the commentaries of the Apostles or the writings of the Prophets, heard the sermon, and all together poured out common prayers, celebrated the sacred Eucharist, at the end of which the presiding one prayed, and the people replied Amen*.

*Basil* (in his rules briefer, q. 178, *De more* etc.): *if our hidden man is not heard, we should abstain from alien parts. What is this that we ought to understand, if not that we should sing with human reason, not as a voice of birds. For* parrots, crows, magpies, and such birds are often taught by men to sound what they do not know. *But to sing knowingly is granted to human nature by the divine will.*

**6.** *This truth is even admitted by some Papist writers*, who prefer the use of the *vulgar tongue* in prayers over the *unknown*. Of *Cardinal Cajetan* we heard above. *Nicholas of Lyra* on Paul in 1 Cor 14 writes to teach, that a *known language is to be used in public prayer*, because if the people understand the prayer or the *benediction of the priest*, better is he reduced to God, and more devoutly responds *Amen*. *Thomas* on the same chapter of the Apostle Paul confesses, that in the primitive Church it was *insanity* if anyone said prayers in an unknown language, because they were then *ignorant* in ecclesiastical rite, not knowing what was done there. The same *Harding* (tom. 1, sect. 28) admits — they acknowledge therefore that the custom of the primitive Apostolic Church differs from the modern rite of the Roman Church: yet no reason is added, since that Epistle was written not only to the Corinthians alone, but *to all Churches of every age*. *Occhus* (*Enchirid.* c. 37) admits it was *conceded to the Austral Indians, that in their language they could perform divine service*: why therefore should not prayers in the customary language be granted also to other peoples? *Aeneas Sylvius*, later called Pope Pius II, writes in his *History of Bohemia* (c. 12): a certain *Cyrillus* asked from the Roman Pope *indult of the Slavonic tongue* in that kingdom, in the public sacred rites; concerning which the matter was disputed long in the sacred senate, and finally the business was decided *by a voice, as it were fallen from heaven*: *let every spirit praise the Lord, and every tongue confess him* — whence Cyril was granted the indult. But we do not require these as proof of our institute.

**If anyone objects the custom of our Churches**, where at times, especially on more solemn days, *collects or public prayers and songs* are heard in the Latin tongue — to be known is: this is not done everywhere, much less in the villages, but only in the more noble cities, where youth in schools have *exercitations of languages* — and where if not all, still some of the auditors *can say Amen*.

**Nor is this custom retained as a part of the divine cult** *out of necessity*, but is an *indifferent rite*, which Paul did not prohibit — since he wished all to speak with tongues (1 Cor 14:5), provided they understood what they were speaking. And this use has this peculiar advantage, that youth may learn for themselves the *Davidic psalms* and other pious cantica long before the Papacy in the temples of Christians in use — which cause is defended by the *Apology of the Augsburg Confession* (Art. 13 and 14). Of which see further in commentary on 1 Cor c. 14 (part 1, q. 3, p. 704–765).

### Case XI. Whether in the prayers of Christians a certain *place* is to be observed

**Respondeo.** *In the Old Testament the sacred cult was, as it were, bound and tied down by certain external circumstances*, among which *place* was one. For God willed prayers to be made at *Jerusalem*, since there was the *temple* and the *propitiatory*, above which God spoke with the people and heard his prayers: therefore he calls it *house of prayer* (Isa 56:7). In which *Solomon* willed prayers to be made, of which he asked the hearing (2 Chr 6:20) — of which *Isaiah* speaks, that it was built up, that thither the *tribes might ascend, to confess the name of the Lord Jehovah* — not because that place was in itself more religious or holy than others, but partly because *there he had placed the memorial of his name* (Exod 20:24), partly because the human body of Christ was a *type* of the *whole universe* (Ephesians, *De incarnatione universi* Heb 8:1). For which cause David in exile anxiously desired the return to the temple (Ps 42:1); and in the beginning of the New Testament, they *went up into this temple for the sake of praying* (Luke 1:9, Luke 18:10). And if any could not be at Jerusalem, they indeed prayed elsewhere, but *with face turned toward the temple*, in which manner Daniel is read to have prayed at his open windows facing toward the temple (Dan 6:10).

**But since now in the New Testament the temple of Jerusalem has been destroyed, and the Levitical ceremonies abrogated, no necessity forces us, no mandate urges, that we should pray in temples at least, or with face turned toward the temple** — but is now fulfilled the vaticination of Malachi (1:11): *in every place shall sacrifice be made, and shall be offered to my name pure oblation*. Whence Paul writes (1 Tim 2:8): *I will therefore that men pray in every place*. And Christ (John 4:21): *the hour cometh, when neither in this mountain, nor at Jerusalem shall ye adore the Father*. And he commands those about to pray to enter *into their chamber, and shut their door* (Matt 6:5–6). *Place does not sanctify prayers, but prayers place*.

*Christ prayed in the garden* (Matt 26:39); *the Apostles in a private house* (Acts 1:14, 12:5, 12); *Paul and Silas in a prison* (Acts 16:25). Let no one therefore be solicitous about the *place of prayer*, provided the mind is sufficiently prepared for praying. Whence *Origen* (vii, *Contra Celsum*) writes: *the Christian, even an idiot most persuaded, thinks that every place in the world is part of the temple of God, in which if one prays with senses shut but with lifted lights of the soul, he ascends over the whole world*.

**In the temple however not only public prayers are rightly instituted, but also *privately the heart is poured out to God*** — not because there is any religion or sanctity in the place, but because the *chief part of divine cult is prayer*, and the presence of many devout men *kindles devotion in those praying*; and the *consent of those praying over one and the same petition has the hope of the promise* (Matt 18:19). Therefore the Church used to *come together for prayers* (Acts 12), which practice was retained in later times, as *Tertullian* writes (*Apologeticum* c. 39): *when we come together in an assembly, as with united hand we bind God — this violence is grateful to God*. And *Augustine* refers (ii, *De doctrina Christiana*) that *the first believers served the Lord in the old temple*. *Pliny the Second* also wrote to Trajan (Ep. 97) that *Christians were accustomed on a fixed day before light to come together, singing a canticle to Christ as it were to God between themselves in turn*.

Since these things are so, we cannot assent to those who reject the *use of temples* altogether, thinking that spiritual men should be content with prayers which they might discharge in chapels and *private oratories* — as of the *Messalians* Damascene refers (Haer. 80); or those who hold that prayers in temples are more sacred and are more easily heard, on account of the presence of God and the mediator Christ in the Eucharist, as the Papists think (Bellarmine iii, *De cultu sanctorum* c. 4; Stapleton *Promtuarium catholicum* on Dom. 9 post Pentecost, q. 1).
