For though God everywhere hears prayers, yet temples are *not to be contemned*, nor are prayers, especially public, in them to be neglected. And though it be useful to pray in temples, yet no *peculiar sanctity* is to be attached to the place, nor is God to be tied to temples, nor is the corporal presence of Christ in the Eucharist outside its *use* to be imagined — but on account of *consent of many*, which kindles ardor in prayers, in temples one should willingly pray; in which sense *Leo* rightly wrote (Sermon on *fast of the seventh month*): *devotion is more efficacious and more holy, when in the works of piety the whole Church is of one mind and effects*.

### Case XII. Whether in prayers a certain *time* is to be observed

**Respondeo.** Also in this kind, all *superstition* is to be avoided: for no *time* for prayer is defined in Scripture, but this was left to the will and *arbiter* of each — lest anyone from fear or necessity of *obligation* work the good, and in every religion as to time of prayer *not even two are found agreeing*, as *Socrates* said (v, *Historia Ecclesiastica* c. 21).

*Christ wills us always to pray, and never to fail* (Luke 18:1). *Paul* commands us to *pray without ceasing* (1 Thess 5:17); *in every time to persevere in prayers* (Eph 6:18); *to be instant in prayer* (Col 4:2).

By which places moved, the ancient heretics *Euchites and Psalliani* were always intent on prayer, and prayed so much that to those who heard this of them it seemed incredible, as *Augustine* writes (*Ad Quod vult*, Haer. 57).

But these words: (1) *commend devotion of soul*, which ought always to be directed to God — for occasion is never wanting either of preaching God's mercy or of asking his benediction; (2) *praise diligence in prayers*, from which all our works ought to begin and end; (3) *remove the superstitious observation of time*, lest we think one time is holier in itself than another, or that prayers made at this or that time are more grateful to God; (4) *note the sighs, by which we always groan to God, since we need his help and defense at every moment of time* — whence *Basil* writes (in *Sermone de fide*): *let every moment of thy life be an exercitation of piety*. Let each pray therefore as often as necessity presses him, according to that of James (5:13): *is any among you afflicted? let him pray*.

**In the meantime it is not prohibited to choose certain hours for prayer**, for *Daniel* is read to have bent his knees three times a day (Dan 6:10); *David* prayed *at evening, at noon, and in the morning* (Ps 55:17) — whence *Christians* have instituted their ordinary prayers for the day: **morning** when they rise and go to their labors, **midday** at prandium or coena, when they enjoy the creatures of God, **evening** when they commit themselves to sleep. If more hours are chosen, it is proper and useful, provided *superstition* and *opinion of necessity* be absent. For all these are of *free observation*.

**Therefore we cannot praise in all things the *custom of the Papists*, who institute *prayers of forty days*.** For although *we can never pray sufficiently*, yet since fixed days are commanded to be observed *of necessity*, and to it an *opinion of merit* is added, that *devotion loses all grace* — and *the many-wordedness of the gentiles which Christ prohibited* (Matt 6:7) is not far off. For the Apostle testifies, that *discrimination of days, foods, and times*, which is adhibited for *religion*, is *taken away in the N.T.* (Gal 4:10).

**From which foundation we also cannot approve the *canonical or regular hours***, in which clerics discharge only the *pension* of their prayers — of which seven they number, and to each their prayerlets they assign: **matins**, which precedes the rising of the sun; **prime**, which extends from dawn to the third hour of the day; **terce**, from the third hour to the sixth; **sext**, which begins from the sixth hour and lasts to the ninth; **nones**, from the ninth hour to the twelfth; **vespers**, which begins at sunset; and finally **compline**, which follows sunset. Some add an **eighth**, and in order set it as first, which they call *nocturnal vigils* — and this distribution they prove from Ps 119:164: *seven times a day do I praise thee*; and v. 62: *at midnight I will rise to give thanks unto thee*. The *origin of these hours* Polydorus Vergil (vi, *De rer. inventor.* c. 1) traces to *Pope Pelagius*, others to Damasus I, others to *Gregory the Great*. They also cite many testimonies of the *Fathers*, who observed those hours — as Jerome, Basil, Chrysostom, whom *Occhus* (*Enchirid.* on prayer, on canonical hours) cites.

**But though we do not deny, that the ancients had certain hours for praying** — yet no *necessity* they urged, nor did they in *consciences* bind men to the observation of those hours, nor did they enjoin the *office of praying* at those hours only on the *priests or other clerics*; all of which in the Papacy are done. Nor was there in the *public cults of all Churches* a *conformity of those hours* — but the Church always had *liberty* in these matters of deciding, whence great difference of old about these hours in the Churches was, testifies *Socrates* (v, *Historia Ecclesiastica* c. 21).

*Jerome* also writes there are *times in which knees are to be bent to God*, but not from *divine institute*, but from *ecclesiastical tradition* — some namely the **third**, **sixth**, and **ninth**. These hours he writes were observed *after the example of the saints*: for at the *third hour the Holy Spirit descended on the Apostles*; at the *sixth*, Peter wishing to eat went up in the *cenaculum*; at the *ninth*, Peter and John ascended into the temple.

*Tertullian* also writes that the same hours were observed — yet so that liberty be preserved for each. *Understand*, he says, *without any precept of observation, always and every time to pray, all human affairs which distinguish the day, which distinguish businesses, which publicly are noted* — thus also *solemnly* to be discerned in praying: since *Daniel is read to have prayed thrice a day*, or from *excepting* other hours, not of others, but *of more insignia* thence *of the Apostles* — the *third, sixth, and ninth*.

It is clear from these, that the *canonical hours* were not to all the Fathers known, nor in the sense and mode with which the Papists established, observed — and therefore *consciences of those praying to them cannot be bound*.

### Case XIII. Whether at prayer a fixed *formula* of prayers should be adhibited

**Respondeo.** Someone might doubt: for a *prescribed formula* of prayer seems to *enclose the Holy Spirit within certain bounds of words* — therefore it would be better to expound our necessities to God in whatever ways we can, especially since *the Spirit is said to intercede for us with groanings which cannot be uttered* (Rom 8:26) when we know not what or how we should pray.

But there is no cause here for conscience to fluctuate. **For the prayers of the saints have been *formally* relinquished to us for that purpose** — especially the *Davidic Psalms* — that we may address God in the words of the Holy Spirit himself. Christ also prescribed for us a *certain formula* of prayer, which we deservedly retain. Nor are the *formulas of prayers* which are found in *public books* prohibited. For nothing thereby is prescribed to the Holy Spirit — but our *infirmity* is helped, since not all can express their calamities aptly enough. It is better to speak to God in *fixed and settled words*, than to pour out unskillfully whatever comes into the mouth.

*The Psalms of David* were certainly always in use in the primitive Church, as is clear from Dionysius (*De ecclesiastica hierarchia* c. 3). To this some refer the Apostle's word (Eph 5:19; Col 3:16): *speaking to yourselves in psalms, and hymns and spiritual songs*, etc.

**Just as a *fixed formula of prayers* without reason is disapproved, so wrongly is it done, if none should be permitted except those formulas which are in Scripture** — as once at the *Council of Laodicea* (c. 59) it was decreed, *lest private psalms in the Church be read*, that is, lest apart from the *Davidic Psalms* others be recited, as is had in the *Council of Bracara* (i, Can. 12).

Superstitious also it is, that in the Papacy at *nocturnal vigils* *twelve Psalms of David* are sung, no others nor more nor fewer — for they fable, that to this number by *angelic institution* they are bound, an angel once appearing to the monks having sung twelve Psalms continuously, and at their end sung *alleluia*, and been silent, nor further seen or heard. As witness they cite the *Council of Turon* (i, c. 19): *at matins at least twelve Psalms be dispatched, as the statutes of the Fathers prescribed, that at the twelfth Psalm alleluia be sung. Which even an angel by manifesting dictated*. See *Azorius* (tom. 1, *Instit. moral.* x, c. 21).

**But we leave these things to their authors, and think no yoke ought to be imposed on consciences in this kind**; for God does not count our prayers, but weighs them — nor does he attend to the *words*, but to the *affect*, with which one prays. Wherefore let each use the *formula and number* of prayers which necessity seems to require.

### Case XIV. Whether *alien cogitations*, which fall in on those praying, impede the hearing of prayers

**Respondeo.** Although *silence in prayers is required*, human weakness and the astuteness of Satan are great, who — since he knows himself most strongly resisted by prayers — labors in whatsoever manner he can to impede or make them vain. Whence it happens that rarely is anyone in his prayers so attentive that he does not sometimes feel *foreign cogitations*.

These are either *honest indeed, but foreign to the purpose*; or *impure and illicit*. Hence conscience is disturbed, whether our prayers, being made *sinful*, do not — much less obtain what we ask? And this temptation of Satan is *not altogether of nothing*, which as *strongly as possible* we can resist, if standing in prayer we breathe up to God, that he teach us to pray, and direct our tongue and mind, and not permit foreign cogitations to fall on us. For *we know not what we should ask* — he himself therefore of his mercy may suggest these to us, and remove the impediments of true prayer. This is done according to the admonition of Sirach (18:33): *before prayer prepare thy soul, and be not as one who tempteth God*.

Moreover, let effort be made, that the *prayer be brief and nervous*, which succinctly proposes the matter — so that we cannot easily wander in cogitations, since *in many words there is often vanity*: which caused Christ to prohibit *battology* in prayers (Matt 6:7).

Finally, let us be mindful, that even if our prayer *labors with some defects*, yet these do not harm us, if we grieve of them seriously. For God does not act with us according to *merits*, but according to his *great mercy* — therefore since he knows *our infirmity* and the *cunning of Satan*, he pardons those defects: *there is no condemnation to those who are in Christ Jesus* (Rom 8:1), though many things in them are found which could condemn.

Wherefore it will be useful at the end of prayers to deprecate the fault which from our part could make prayers vain — and thus foreign cogitations shall not stand in the way of the hearing of prayers, just as the *venial sins* which yet cling to saints, and to which they permit no dominion, do not impede their salvation.

*So much of Cases of Conscience which fall about prayers.*

— ❦ —

## Chapter VIII. On Cases of Conscience Concerning Vows

*De casibus conscientiae circa vota.*

We progress from prayers to *vows*, which among the Greeks are expressed by one word. For *εὐχή* signifies *both* the postulation by which we desire something in praying, *and* the promise of offering something to God if we have obtained what we sought.

Now vows are *promises made to God, from the judgment of reason and the proposition of the will* — when we judge that we shall do what we promise, and we determine to do it. A vow differs from a promise as species from genus: for a vow is a promise or engagement made *to God*, whereas any promise is called a *pollicitation*, even that which is made to a man. A vow to God can be made without external words as signs; a promise cannot consist without words or signs. A promise can itself be matter of vow: for if thou hast promised some money to a poor man, thou canst moreover vow to God that thou wilt bestow that money on the poor (as Aquinas teaches, IIa IIae q. 88 art. 1).

Much is disputed among the scholastics, whether a vow is an act of reason or of will — but this dispute does not pertain to our institute; rightly however it is referred to both. For in a vow three things are: *deliberation*, which is of reason; *proposition*, which is of will; and *promise*, which is of both — begun by the first, confirmed by the second, consummated by the third. Deliberation first decrees to pledge something to God; proposition establishes to vow to God; promise binds faith, and obliges the man to God.

The *Romanists* add *solemnization*, as they speak, by which one binds himself to the obligation of the vow by a prescribed solemn rite and pact, as monks do.

In the Papacy vows are variously divided: (i) into *solemn and simple* — solemn is with a solemn juridical formula, quality, or condition added (reception of holy orders, or profession of approved religion); simple is any other vow made without a solemn juridical formula; (ii) into *implicit and explicit* — implicit, when one undertakes something which requires a vow (as in ordination to holy orders, the vow of chastity is required by the Papists); explicit, when one expressly obliges himself to God; (iii) into *absolute and conditional* — absolute have no added condition; conditional are affected with a certain condition (as if thou vow to go to holy places, or undertake a certain religion, if thou art restored from disease); (iv) into *temporal and perpetual* — temporal, when for a certain time one binds himself to something (as for a fast of some days); perpetual, which endure to the end of life (as the vow of chastity); (v) into *penal and non-penal* — penal is when one vows something under condition of a certain penalty if he shall not stand by his promises; non-penal has no such penalty adjoined. Of all which see Azorius, tom. i, *Instit.*, lib. 11 c. 1.

But since these species of vows are not all consonant with the divine Word, we do not attend much to all these distinctions.

The *perpetual vow of Christians is Baptism*, in which a man renounces Satan and the world, denies himself, and promises he will serve Christ — whence Baptism is called the *stipulation of a good conscience* (1 Pet 3:21). This vow is daily repeated in the exercises of penitence, and is altogether necessary, as the chief part of divine cult.

Besides this general and perpetual vow, in Scripture three other genera of vows are found. **One of gratitude** — which we may call *εὐχαριστεία*, when one placed in perils, out of gratitude vows something to God his liberator, if he shall be delivered thence, as *Jonah 2*: *the Lord did not despise my prayer*: *when I am set free, I will pay thee, in the great congregation I will return my vows*, *what my mouth spake in tribulation, that will I render* (Ps 66:13). **Another of devotion and piety**, which we may call *religious*, by which our services are vowed to God — such was the vow of David (Ps 132:2): *he swore to the Lord, he vowed a vow to the God of Jacob, I will not enter into the tabernacle of my house*. Hither pertains the vow of the *Nazarites*, by which they consecrated themselves to the Lord with other rites which God himself described (Num 6); and the vow of Hanna, offering her son to God at Shiloh (1 Sam 1:11). **The third is of external pedagogy or legal observance**, which we may call *pedagogical* — by which in the OT they vowed observance of certain days, fastings, etc., of which afterwards.

Since therefore vows have their foundation in Scripture, and are frequented among all nations, and easily one may sin in this part, it is worth the effort to consider those things which can excite the conscience in this genus.

### Case I. Whether we should vow anything to God, or if vows are forbidden to us

**Respondeo.** Nor is this dubitation altogether about nothing. For a fourfold *ratio dubitandi* can be given:

**1.** Because *we already owe to God all our things*, to offer to himself, since the divine precept urges us — so that there is no need of a peculiar obligation from vow.

**2.** Because *what is offered freely to God pleases him more*, since he requires from us a spontaneous cult — but by vow we impose a necessity on ourselves, which can render worshippers unwilling, by which very thing our oblation loses its grace: for *God loveth a cheerful giver* (2 Cor 9:7).

**3.** Because *there is peril of transgression*: for if anyone should not perform what he has vowed, he would bind himself with sin — therefore it were perhaps better not to vow, than to weave a snare for one's conscience.

**4.** Because *there is no mandate in the NT* by which vows are exacted of us, no promise that they are pleasing to God and useful to us.

**Respondeo.** These are not of such moment that they cannot be subjected to consideration. For *to what refers the first*: we owe indeed all our things to God, but often we are more sluggish to perform. Now vows serve to excite our minds, so we may be more prompt to perform what otherwise binds us. So Jacob (Gen 28:22): he vows that he will worship God, to which nevertheless by the force of the first precept he was bound.

*Object*: Then Christ falsely denied vows to be a matter of law (Matt 19:11). *Respondeo*: Certain vows are not a thing commanded elsewhere by the law, but a thing of counsel — but this is thus to be understood: that the *thing* commanded is not the total matter of the vow, but of the precept also; but the *thing that comes under counsel* is the sole matter of the vow, not of law or precept.

*To the second*: vows indeed impose the necessity *of performing*, but not *of coaction*. For as boys who are twisted at studies please God little, but those who are performed with free spirit — although by reason of promise they involve a debt — so vows are performed from a debt, because promise brings a debt with itself. Rightly, however, it is added: *what remains from will and effect in the spirit does not remove necessity*. Necessity of *action* is joined with justice, therefore Paul does not will alms as of justice and necessity, but as free and prompt (2 Cor 9:7). Vows therefore are debited, either because commanded by God, or because we have subjected ourselves to precepts.

*To the third*: distinguish among things which are, from their own nature, base and dishonest — and those which arise from our vice. The first are altogether to be fled and omitted: to seek or vow illicit and dishonest things. But the second are not always to be omitted on account of perils: for food and drink can also bring peril of transgression, not from their own nature but from our vice — if we use them intemperately or immoderately. And yet food and drink are not forbidden on this account. Thus the difficulty about vows is not so great — for as we said, what we vow we already owe to God on other grounds; therefore in vowing we do not make ourselves more indebted. That every peril be shunned in difficulties, it is better to make a vow with condition: *if the Lord shall have willed, and shall have given us strength* — nor should we vow anything prohibited by divine law, or impossible, nor for every time of our life, but only for a time until the peril of danger vanishes.

Finally as to the fourth: it is indeed true that vows pertain rather to the OT — where we have both precepts and examples of vows. In the NT the examples are rare, and precepts almost none. Nowhere however are vows *forbidden* to Christians. In the OT they were part of the divine cult, as of ceremonial things (Sir 18:23); in the NT they are only *subsidies and helps of piety*. In the OT certain vows were necessary, as vows of sacrifices and oblations, and when houses and fields were consecrated to the use of the temple. Such vows properly pertained to the OT and do not bind us today, since all ceremonies are removed through Christ.

In the NT they are altogether free, as one may see (Acts 5:4): what Peter said to Ananias, *while it remained, did it not remain thine? and after it was sold, was it not in thine own power?* Yet vows still — since they confirm the mind in the Lord, and check us from ill-doing, provided they have nothing else superstitious about them — of which afterwards.

**Note therefore**: licit vows have matter of three genera: **1.** *Observance of the mandates of God*; **2.** *Flight of sins* — such is the vow we made in Baptism, which we call *stipulation*. This is the chief part of divine cult, and always must be our proposition — to serve those things which we have promised to God in Baptism. Therefore *Luther called Baptism alone the vow of Christian religion, in which is contained the sum of all vows, whereby we are firmly bound to render to God the cult expressly prescribed in his Word* (*Sermo de sacro baptismo*, tom. i, Jena German, fol. 279); **3.** *Bodily exercises*, such as fasting, giving alms, abstinence from a certain food or drink for the sake of temperance. Such vows may today also be made, provided they are made from Christian liberty (not with opinion of cult), as helps to prayers, labors, meditations, and other pious rites. No cult, however, consists in them — since *the kingdom of God is not meat and drink, but righteousness and peace* (Rom 14:17).

### Case II. Concerning one who vows something to God under saints

**Respondeo.** This dubitation the Papists move, from which they easily could extricate themselves if they were self-consistent. For the scholastics expressly teach that vows are made *to God alone* — and by this very thing they distinguish a vow from a promise, since the former is only to God, the latter to man.

But from this scholastic doctrine the *later* have departed. For Bellarmine expressly writes (i, *De cult. sanct.*, c. 15) that *a saint may be regarded to whom a vow is directly made*. In the institutions of religion, when the religious solemnly profess their rule, they call it *a vow made to God under the saints* — as Augustine has it: *thou hast made me a servant, thou hast made me a servant of that mother, of Iuvenal, of Paula, of Sulpicia, of Barbara, of Rufina*.

We cannot approve all which, and we establish it to be *an impious vow, since it is not made to God alone*. For since vows are exacted in Scripture, express mention is made only of God (Deut 23:22; Ps 50:14; Ps 76:12); and God alone has right of animadverting on those who violate vows; and vows are a certain species of *confession* and *religious invocation*, which we owe to God alone. As therefore in prayers we conjoin no saints, so in vows also — evil are joined saints departed.

The Papists, seeing this truth, therefore soften their opinion of vows made to saints, and say: a promise made to saints or prelates falls under a vow materially, insofar as a man vows to God to perform what he promises to saints or prelates (Aquinas IIa IIae q. 88 art. 5). But to saints departed we cannot promise anything, since they neither know the recesses of our hearts, nor hear what is promised, nor can help nor vindicate if promises are not observed. But if the performance of what is promised to living men is vowed to God, if the things are licit, that vow is not evil — but in it men are not joined to God in the matter of the vow, about which there is no controversy.

Others say: a vow is made to God in the *presence* of the saints, who are held as witnesses, advocates, and patrons, so that we may perform what we promise more properly and commodiously (Bellarmine, *De sanct. beat.*, i c. 12).

But (1) this explanation does not agree with the *form* of a vow, in which something is vowed *to God*, according to Augustine and others; (2) even the departed do not hear us, nor have cognizance of our institutes — how therefore can they be called as witnesses? (3) God does not need witnesses, since he searches hearts and reins, and can animadvert on those who violate the vow.

The same Azorius says: vows indeed are made to God, but to the saints as ministers of God who hold his place — vows are made to God properly and in his name, but to men figuratively in the name of God. But by this reasoning also to the magistrate, parents, and others who hold God's place, vows should be pronounced — when this is part of his glory, which he does not will to give to another (Isa 42:8); nor anywhere does God will him to make vows in his name, nor have we examples of any who did this.

*A Papist figment therefore*, and evilly made judgment, which never can consolidate conscience. Wherefore let us either omit vows made to saints altogether, or make them to God alone.

### Case III. Who can rightly vow, and by whom vows can be made to God

**Respondeo.** Since a vow ought to be made from proposition and deliberation, and, as Aquinas teaches, those cannot vow who are demented or infants — who do not understand the nature of the vow — by the law of nature they are barred from vowing.

There are also others to whom it is prohibited by divine law to vow, as those who are *of their own right* — but yet are under the power of others. For of these we read (Num 30:6) that if the father contradict the vow of a daughter in her age of puberty, he can render it null (vv. 6, 8). And of the vows of the espoused (v. 7), and of the husband over the vows of his wife (v. 11). In which place no mention is made of *sons of families* and *slaves*, but the same reasoning applies to them; because God gave the power of the father over the son, and of the master over the slave, that under pretext of a vow they should not bind themselves. There are also monks, since they are under another power, therefore likewise in general without consent of superiors they should not vow.

This is to be understood of vows which impede their duty, subjection to superiors, or their obedience; or which impede works, or concern certain bodily exercises (fasts or abstinence from certain things). In these they cannot without consent of superiors — since they are not of their own right. But in works of piety which fall under prayers, alms, and similar things, they can perform even without consent of superiors — since in these they are bound not to men but to God, who exacts these works of piety from them, and it is not verisimilar that superiors will oppose in these things. Provided in this the *necessity of vowing* be shunned: for burdens of piety are performed even without vow, but for the sake of greater excitation of soul a vow can accede. But let *opinion of merit be absent* — for works of piety are not meritorious but rather owed obligation.

Here the scholastics dispute: *if a husband consents to a wife's vow, can he afterwards rescind it?* Negatively answers *Cajetan on IIa IIae q. 88 art. 8*, from Num 30:14. Others (since husband and wife are equals) affirm — Sotus, *De just.*, lib. 7 q. 5 art. 3, gl. in c. *Manifestum* q. 5. Sotus says: *the vow of the husband and wife I make similar, since the husband is head of the wife*. The latter opinion seems more common to us. Since among spouses is the closest communion of things, it were most equitable that in this thing also there be mutual latitude of consent to what is instituted.

They dispute also: *whether abbots, prelates, monks, or nuns, once profession is emitted, can vow anything for themselves?* Of which see Azorius, tom. i, *Instit.*, lib. 11 c. 16. But since we have shown that monastic profession is not from God but of human invention — contrary to divine ordinance in many things — we do not delay in these disputes.

**Concerning vows of priests and ministers of the Church**, they are to be treated together with other Christians, so that peculiarly of them there is no need.

**We conclude therefore**: **1.** Those can vow who are *of their own right*, either by reason of age or by reason of state and condition. Whence it follows that it is done ill in the Papacy, that to monastic vows those are not so much admitted as forced, who — youths not yet through the fifteenth year of age completed (which by ancient right was licit — Azorius, IIa IIae q. 88 art. 8). Which the Council of Trent perceived, and altered the ancient custom, decreeing that monastic profession should not be made before the sixteenth year of age (Sess. 25 c. 15). But not even this can consult consciences. For that age which the little Council of Trent has judged sufficient to bear that burden, is still slippery and unsuited to perform what scarcely those of firmest age can perform. But to slippery age faith should not be easily granted — as Chrysostom writes on 1 Tim c. 5.

These the ancients well understood, and thus the *Council of Carthage* c. 3 (and *Justinian*, *Novell.* 63) prohibited the veiling of a virgin before the fortieth year of age; and the *Roman* Council c. 10 willed monks not to be consecrated before the 25th year; and of virgins *whom the Aristo says*, they should not be blessed before the 62nd year, for the reason added that after that age there is no peril lest their chastity be violated. But from these ordinances of the ancients, the Romanists long since departed.

**2.** Those can vow who in vowing contain themselves *within the limits of their own vocation* — if by the vow they are impeded in their vocation, they are not sufficient to vow. Whence it follows: vows of pilgrimages to holy places, which are made for the sake of religion, are not licit — since impediment of business owed to us by reason of our vocation and things committed to us, is not licit. For by this reasoning the kind of life divinely commanded to us is deserted; and a new one, which relies on no Word, is taken up — which cannot be done without wound of conscience. For to all it is said (1 Cor 7:20): *let every one abide in the calling wherein he was called*.

### Case IV. Whether promiscuously we may vow whatever pleases us

**Respondeo.** The matter of a licit vow is not arbitrary, but limited by certain limits. For we are not rashly to vow to God that which either is impious to perform, or is impossible — for this were to mock God. Let this therefore be the rule: *the matter of a vow ought to be some virtue, or some other thing good, honest, and honorable in itself*. Whence is collected what is not to be vowed.

**Not to be vowed**: **(1)** *sin, or an illicit thing prohibited by God's express Word* — such was the vow of the Jews (Acts 23:12, 14), who bound themselves neither to eat nor drink till they had killed Paul. Of this kind: if thou vow to kill or wound someone, or for a certain time not to give alms — hither pertains the Papal vow of killing a king, in his opinion a heretic. So the *Lord* would not receive the reward of a harlot, or the price of a dog, though vowed to him (Deut 23:18). *For if the mind ought to remain, that thou wilt do what thou hast vowed, thou hast made an impious and illicit vow*. Such a vow is: if one vowed that until his tenth year he would give no alms to Christ. Hither pertains the vow of the Judaeans mentioned above.

**(2)** *A light and inept thing* — as if one vowed on the Sabbath, or on a certain day, that he would not remove a straw or a stalk. Hither pertain what Azorius rehearses in his *Instit. moral.*, lib. 11 c. 16, num. 15, as examples: a little woman vowed not to eat on the Sabbath, for the honor of the Mother of God; another vowed not to eat heads of animals, for the cult and honor of John the Baptist; or not to eat flesh roasted with fire, out of veneration of Saint Lawrence Martyr. Which vows, since they are superstitious, he too disapproves (loc. cit. c. 16 num. 3). But he *approves* if a woman vowed to abstain from all servile work on the Sabbath, for the cult of the Mother of God, or if she made a vow not to eat flesh for the cult of Saint John or Saint Lawrence. But equally stupid is this vow as the prior, partly because servile work on the Sabbath is required by certain necessity, which vow could impede, partly because in honor of no saint should something be vowed, since the cult of these saints themselves is illicit.

Hither pertains that noble old dubium about the *vow of Jephthah* (Judg 11:31), *whatsoever cometh forth of the doors of my house to meet me, when I return in peace from the children of Ammon, shall surely be the Lord's, and I will offer it up for a burnt offering*. And when he had first seen his daughter meet him, he rent his clothes vehemently perturbed — that already by his rash vow he had bound himself. That his daughter he did not immolate, but consecrated to perpetual virginity, is held by some, whom Lyra follows, and the Rabbi *Perkinsius*, and others.

The Jesuit Azorius embraces indeed the prior opinion, but only on account of the consent of the Fathers — if this did not oppose, he says, he would rather approve the latter opinion (i, *Instit.*, lib. 11 c. 16). Truly, however, that we may less subscribe to the prior opinion, other certain evident things oppose: nor from Scripture, nor from other suitable writers, can it be proved that in the OT the vow of *perpetual virginity* was used among the people of God. Whence to *hazard* an institution, as if it had already been in use in that time, is not slight — but of virgins this cannot be shown, that by vow they consecrated themselves to perpetual virginity: for *sterility* among the Jews was accounted contumelious and a mark of malediction (Exod 23:26, Deut 7:14); nay not even today among the Jews is such vow of virginity known.

Those who follow the latter opinion first rely on the ambiguity of the Hebrew text at v. 31, where in the Vulgate version we have: *I will offer him as a whole burnt offering to the Lord*; but in the sources it is read: *and I will offer him to the Lord, or as a whole burnt offering I will offer to the Lord* — which words are read disjunctively: either I will offer to the Lord, if it is such which can be offered, or otherwise consecrated to the Lord (for many things could occur which could not be offered or immolated, as unclean animals — but money brought to the temple was to be redeemed).

But this reason of interpreting the text through disjunction is not necessary, since it can be expounded copulatively also — therefore the LXX interpreters omitted the latter conjunction altogether, so they rendered: *whatsoever cometh forth to me shall be the Lord's, and I will offer it as a burnt offering*. Moreover ET is taken for VEL in two distinct quasi-species, as Gen 26:11: *he that shall touch this man or his wife*, and Exod 21:15: *he that smiteth his father or his mother*.

But they say: to *immolate men was not licit by law*. Yet this in no way opposes the prior opinion. For for this very cause the vow is held as rash, that *Jephthah* is reprehended by the Chaldee Paraphrast, because he did not consult *Phinehas* the priest before he performed what he vowed. Some think even Phinehas is at fault, because he did not receive Jephthah when he came, and admonish that the vow could have been dissolved at a price.

But (they say) Jephthah was ruled by the Holy Spirit (v. 29), and by faith is said to have obtained the victory (Heb 11:32); how therefore could he pollute himself by so cruel a vow? Which reasoning has moved some to establish: in Jephthah's vow there was no sin, since led by the impulse of the Holy Spirit he vowed and paid — which is the opinion of *Occolampadius, Senarius on Judges*, q. 11, fol. 336.

But I do not see how, this opinion standing, God, as if delighted with human slaughter by credulity, could absolve. For *homicide* is what God himself in the Law forbade — never has he commanded it, or instigated it: for when he willed Abraham to immolate his son, it was a *temptation or probation*, not a precept — for never seriously did the Lord will it, therefore he permitted him at the very act. That the Holy Spirit is written to have led Jephthah, does not have this sense: that from the instinct of the Holy Spirit he made this vow and paid it; but that he stirred up this hero as an enemy, and imparted victory to him — which even without vow could have happened. Jephthah had faith, but the faith of the saints has many imperfections, as *Sarah*: *Abraham* and *David* had the Holy Spirit, and yet the one doubted, the other polluted himself with concubinage. Paul's *argument from the exempla of the saints* therefore proves nothing.

Others hold Jephthah *consecrated his daughter to perpetual virginity* — because the girl asked her father for leave to bewail her virginity on the mountains (v. 37), since by her father's vow she was cut off from marriage forever, and had not known a man (v. 39): but *sterility* was accounted a malediction (Exod 23:26, Deut 7:14, Ps 128:3) — which she bewailed. But if she had been about to be immolated, she would have bewailed not so much virginity as life.

But this reasoning is infirm. For that she saw her father's vow was to be irrevocable and she was to die, vehemently she was bewailing her most sad condition — that she must depart from the living, before she should see her offspring — through whom not only should the memory of her name be preserved among the people of God, but the true cult of God be propagated: which was among the promises of God the greatest vow.

They say also: from the fact that yearly the penalty to remember Jephthah's daughter is enough to condescend that she was not immolated. In the Hebrew text (v. 40) it is read that the daughters of Israel came together *לתנות* (letanoth), which voice in the Latin version is rendered *to lament*; but it can be more aptly rendered *to converse* or *to speak together* — whence they collect the daughters came together to consolation in speech about her.

But there is no necessity of this version. For the Hebrew voice *tanah* in its second signification signifies *dono dedit* (gave in gift) — which is general and can be understood of lamentation and conversation of what was granted to the girl. Most interpreters in this place render *to lament*; the Chaldee Paraphrast has the voice *lealah*, which is from the root of *alah*, which without doubt signifies *to lament*; Rabbi Solomon and *Onkelos* render *to lament*; the LXX have *θρηνεῖν*, and so *Luther* also.

*Since therefore there is no reason which solidly convinces that Jephthah did not immolate his daughter, but only consecrated her to perpetual virginity, we conclude*: Jephthah was to be reprehended, so also to which errors some some soften with the sentence Jephthah, partly reduced to gentile custom (who sometimes sacrificed their children to their gods), partly by the example of *Abraham*, whom he willed to immolate his son — persuading himself he could do the same, though it was forbidden by divine law. But neither of these excuses Jephthah: for to follow gentile customs was a piaculum for the Israelitic people, whom for that very cause God sequestrated from the gentiles, that they should not follow the customs of the gentiles (Lev 20:22). And the example of *Abraham* also cannot be used, since he used a special mandate of God — not to be proposed for imitation. Now the general precept was: no men should be immolated (Lev 18:21).

Such therefore was the error committed in this vow, which should have been expiated by penitence — which even Jephthah is believed to have done, since his name is placed among those whose faith is preached in the Epistle to the Hebrews (11:32).

**(3)** *A thing otherwise impossible* — as if one vowed never to sin. We may indeed *wish* this, but we cannot *vow* it, since on account of the weakness of the flesh we shall never perform it. So indeed one who vows to make general confession of all sins to a priest — Aquinas holds this for a licit vow (IIa IIae q. 88 art. 6). But Scripture places it among impossibilia, saying *there is not a just man upon earth, that doeth good, and sinneth not* (Eccl 7:20).

**(4)** *A thing which will otherwise happen, or is necessary* — as if one vows that after the sun rises, he will resume his labors. Vain is such vow, in which the name of the Lord is taken in vain. Such vows differ little from those foolish and inept ones we mentioned above, which have no utility, and are sometimes even joined with baseness.

**(5)** *What cannot be done without peril of soul or body* — as immodest fastings, flagellations of the body, watches with long intermissions of the night, and similar. When a vow proceeds from a good end, it should not induce peril.

### Case V. Whether one can vow to God that he will avoid every genus of sin

**Respondeo.** We have said above: no one, in this corrupted nature, can flee *every* sin — therefore neither ought he to bind himself by vow to this. Another reasoning however is, if inquiry is about a certain species of sin.

The scholastics hold that a vow of every venial sin has no strength — for one to flee every venial sin is a gift of special divine grace. But a vow of fleeing every mortal sin, they say, is fixed and firm — since the benefit of divine grace, by which one can abstain from every mortal sin, is not unusual with God, although it is a great gift of God.

But of a *particular* sin they say one can vow: to flee a certain genus of venial sin — although some genera of venial sin are difficult to know and to avoid; hence the flight of them is not to be vowed. If one has vowed such flight, it should be commuted, or relaxed (a disputation to be read at length in *John Azorius*, tom. i, *Instit.*, lib. 11 c. 17).

But if we consult sacred Scripture, we are taught abundantly that *the flight of every sin — not only mortal, but even venial — is impossible for us in this corrupt nature*. And although the Papists produce the example of the 7 virgins of the Blessed Virgin, of whom they suppose one to be born even without original sin, and to have lived without mortal sin — this itself is destitute of all foundation, as elsewhere is taught by profession.

Scripture teaches, *that which is born of flesh is flesh* (John 3:6). *Every figment of man's heart is intent on evil at every time* (Gen 6:5). Hence *God concluded all under sin, that he might have mercy upon all* (Rom 11:32). *There is not a just man on earth, that doeth good, and sinneth not* (Eccl 7:20; 1 Kgs 8:46). Whence *Augustine* rightly (*De spir. et lit.* c. 16): *he has made much progress, who has known how far he is from the perfection of justice, by progressing knew*.

Since therefore no one can avoid every sin, it were rash to bind oneself to this matter by vow — this would be the same as to vow that thou wilt no longer pray for remission of sins; but this prayer has no place where there is no sin.

It can happen, however, that one firmly propose to himself to flee a certain species of sin — as drunkenness, fornication, strifes and quarrels with the neighbor, avarice, etc. If anyone feels himself more prone to such sin, he can oppose the bars of a vow to his desires, so he may the more diligently beware of what he is otherwise compelled to avoid by divine law. For such a vow is nothing else than a *firm proposition of soul* to flee a certain sin — which lest it easily slip from us, nor let us fall if that proposition trip us up, the vow forbids, that our soul be contained in duty.

Of this genus is that which *Sirach* wishes: *let a custody be set over my mouth, and above my lips a certain seal, that I fall not from them, and my tongue destroy me not* (Sir 22:33) — which are very like vows.

But if this in the vow seems difficult to the flesh, let it be procured by prayers, that God, who gave the soul of vowing what is good and honest, may also grant strength to perform. For the grace of God to flee a certain special sin is not *ἀπόδραστον* (something to be evaded): God has granted grace to many of abstaining from drunkenness, from avarice, and other pollutions, though from other things they were altogether unable.

### Case VI. Whether indifferent things fall under a vow

**Respondeo.** Indifferent things are all contingents, which can be otherwise than they turn out. Of these, common to us is the doctrine of the scholastics: that indifferent things, considered simply and in themselves, do not fall under vow. For it were vain to vow not to accede to a banquet, not to enter a certain house, not to contract friendship with anyone — for all these are in the number of the licit, and should not be impeded by vow.

But by reason of *some special circumstance*, it can happen that such a vow becomes fixed and firm — for example, if one vowed not to go to banquets to avoid drunkenness or vain discourses, or not to enter such a house to avoid occasion of sinning, or not to contract friendship with someone lest he be seduced. Of which genus many things occur in common use.

[Here holds that saying: *it is safer to avoid the occasion of sinning.*]

Occasion of evil therefore makes a vow, which of itself is vain and irritating, become just, ratified, and firm. When however the occasion of evil ceases, then that thing is no longer matter of the vow, but begins again to be an indifferent means.

### Case VII. Can one make a vow to God concerning contracting matrimony with some person, or on the contrary of not entering matrimony ever?

**Respondeo.** In the doctrine of this case the scholastics disagree.

Some think that *matrimony*, since it is a natural function, cannot be matter of a vow, since it impedes the state of perfection — which they suppose *celibacy* to be, which those profess who have entered any religion, or are initiated to sacred orders. Those therefore who vow matrimony, do the same as if they vowed not to ascend to a higher grade of virtue. If however matrimony is a *remedy of libido*, then it can fall under a vow — since as Paul witnesses, *it is better to marry than to burn* (1 Cor 7:9). Him therefore in whom libido dominates, and whose chastity is often shaken by allurements, they concede can vow marriage. So Aquinas, IIa IIae q. 88 art. 3; and Cajetan on IIa IIae q. 88 art. 1.

The contrary is held by *Sotus* (lib. 7 *De iust.* q. 1 art. 3): matrimony is not matter of a vow, not even insofar as it is remedy of libido — that is, not even for one who is more incontinent can he vow to contract matrimony. For although he who contracts matrimony medicates libido, yet the vow of matrimony does not bring remedy for libido.

We do not make that controversy our own.

That the conscience be satisfied, we presuppose: *every vow ought to have a good end* — and a good end is that which is not against the Word or ordinance of God, and which aids the utility of the neighbor, or is also profitable to ourselves. Matrimony therefore can be vowed, even insofar as it is a *natural function*, if for a good end one asks such vows. For that it *impede the state of perfection* is an idle hypothesis — since scarcely such men are more perfect than the holy Patriarchs, who nevertheless lived in marriage and begot children (Gen 25). Nor is it credible that a human invention (as a certain profession of religion, and perpetual celibacy) has greater perfection than the ordination of God, which is marriage.

Honest causes therefore can occur, on account of which one binds himself by vow to contract matrimony, even outside danger of dominating libido — as if one is compelled by others to *perpetual celibacy as a state of more perfect being*, in which there is great merit, and indeed greater than in marriage; then he can vow that he will contract matrimony as swiftly as possible, that by that very fact he may contradict this impious opinion. Or if his celibacy is being made suspicious to others, though without fault of his own — for we must consult not only conscience but also reputation. Or if the salvation of the state or something similar hangs on it, as sometimes happens; or if some girl can be rescued from the hands of heretics or tyrants, or from suspicion of dishonest life; or if there is hope, that through such marriage someone may be edified in life and morals, and even helped in matters of faith and salvation (as Peter testifies, 1 Pet 3:1–2: *ye wives, be in subjection to your own husbands, that if any obey not the word, they may be won by the conversation of the wives*).

In such case one can bind himself by vow to God to contract marriage with a certain person. For what is *occasion of some good* — which promotes both temporal and eternal felicity of ourselves as of others; which can be *remedy* to avoid sins — that certainly may be vowed. And so also stands the vow about matrimony to be contracted, insofar as it is *a function of nature*.

Since however it is *remedy of libido*, much more can it be vowed by those who feel the dominion of vicious affections over themselves — since this is nothing else than *to remove the occasion of sinning*, which can be impeded more swiftly by vow than by proposition of soul alone. But we in the genus attend, not what ought to be done, or is necessary, but only what is licit, if one wishes to do it.

Otherwise stands the case, when the vow is directed *to a quality of person, or to some less honest end* — as if one vowed to contract a noble or rich or beautiful wife: for these are indifferent things, which as we said above, do not fall under matter of vow. Or if one vowed to take a wife not from desire of offspring, but only for the sake of extinguishing libido; for this end is illicit. For as Raphael said to Tobias (Tob 6:17): *the devil has power over those who so enter into matrimony, that God is excluded, and follow their lust, as horses and mules which have no understanding*. If one vowed to contract matrimony with some person, that he might poison her, or attach her to a plague, or make her a partner of his sins, it were an iniquitous vow, and least to be conceded; nor can it oblige, since its ends are all illicit.

From all these it is clear enough what is to be established about vows about contracting matrimony.

The vow however which is *against* matrimony is plainly not to be made: as if one vowed he would never take a wife, though he was apt for marriage. For such a vow is contrary to God's ordinance, and joined with peril of soul or body, since one prohibits himself the licit remedies by which conjugium could cure libido. Such vows should not be made, which oppose God's order, wound body or soul, or offer occasion of sinning.

But if one vowed to abstain from marriage *at least for a time*, until he has instituted a more honest kind of life, or provided for his other things, or convalesced from disease, or returned from some journey — such a vow is licit, and sometimes necessary, for those who by juvenile impetus or by the immature persuasions of friends are drawn to matrimony, and might otherwise involve themselves with great peril of untimely marriage.

### Case VIII. Whether it is licit to vow some good things which impede greater goods

**Respondeo.** The matter of a vow ought to be *good, honest, and useful*: but since there are degrees of goodness, it can happen that one doubts whether by vowing some good he has placed an obstacle to a better. *Exempli gratia*: someone by vow has committed himself to some honest function — but afterwards a sublimer office is offered to him, in which he could preside with praise: is asked here, *does the vow oblige him, so that with good conscience he must relinquish the office insofar as he vowed it, and cannot ascend to loftier?*

**Respondeo**: This vow has force, nor is it easily to be rescinded — for it obliges, since it is honest and licit. Nor is it necessary in a vow that always the *optimum* be vowed; it suffices that it is *good in itself* and useful to others. When one is bound by vow to some office, he must remain, until by those whom he is serving he is dismissed — for then the obligation of the vow expires, when its performance is no longer within his power; and here holds that saying: *ultra posse nemo obligatur*. Yet always the soul and proposition of serving those to whom we are bound by vow should remain — as *Samuel*, though rejected from his political office by the people, said nevertheless: *far be this sin from me, that I should cease to pray for you, and to teach you the good and right way* (1 Sam 12:23) — for to this he had been bound by his mother's vow (1 Sam 1:11).

When therefore anyone rests quiet in the function to which he devoted himself, even if called to loftier, by reason of his vow he can neither aspire to nor accept the vocation offered.

**Exception**, however, is *extreme necessity*: as in defect of others who are sufficiently apt for that sublimer office — where God himself dispenses in the vow, lest the public commodity of many be impeded. For when one is bound by vow to some good, tacitly is understood this condition: *provided the glory of God or public commodity be not impeded by this vow*. For when this happens, that vow is held for nothing — since it involves an illicit thing, when against the glory of God and the public commodity nothing is to be vowed.

Cautious therefore let us be in vows of this kind, lest we throw difficulties upon ourselves. For here again we attend not what ought to be done, but what, if one wishes to do it, is licit, and when it is done can be excused in conscience.

Let it be noted, however, that the scholastics in deciding this question compare vows about any other good with the *religious life*. For they hold monastic life to be *good of all goods most excellent*, since to it they ascribe the *state of perfection*, and therefore they think nothing else should be vowed, if in its own genus it is optimum, which impugns the monastic life (so *Cajetan* on IIa IIae q. 88 art. 2, and *Anthonius* p. 3 tit. 7 c. 12; *Palutanus* on 4 *Distinct*. 38 art. 3 q. 1 &c., of which see *Azorius*, lib. 11 *Instit. moral.* c. 24 q. 7).

But they err in the hypothesis — that the *religious life*, such as they profess in the Papacy, is the most perfect good; when it is only a human invention, as we shall show in its place. Many things can be vowed in things divinely instituted (as matrimony, or some public function) — in which the vow cannot be rendered null by subsequent profession of religion, since divine things do not yield to human. About the *relaxation* of vows we shall speak a little later, and shall explicate them more fully.

*Thus much of the material of vows.* Now follow other things which pertain to the *formal reasoning* of vows.

### Case IX. Whether even vows made from fear and perturbation of soul are valid

**Respondeo.** There are found men who — terrified by grave disease, shipwreck, robbers, or some other sudden peril — vow many things: who, lest they be afterwards driven in conscience, cautiously should note the following.

**There are those who think all vows made from a sudden and inconsidered event to be null**: a vow ought to be made from a full and deliberate proposition of the soul, which in perturbed men cannot exist. Therefore also *penitence* — nay, such a vow seems extorted from the unwilling; but since God wills a spontaneous worshipper, a vow of this kind does not seem accepted by him. In which opinion agrees *St. Antoninus* (part 2 tit. 4 c. 15), and *Gregory the Great* (24 c. 34); on that basis to the Papal decree, one terrified by fear of death and vowing the state of religion, the vow is relaxed.

**Others hold the contrary**, thinking any vow whatsoever made under whatever affect to be valid — since the perturbing affects do indeed perturb the man, but do not take away every judgment of reason.

*Sciendum est*: there is one *light and vain fear*, which does not fall on a constant man — and there is no doubt, that a vow made from such fear is not valid, since the judgment of reason is not much perturbed, as if from fear of things which cannot harm, one vows some things absurdly, from which he might be liberated. But there is another *grave and just fear*, which does fall on a constant man — as when one suffers a smaller injury or expects a jactura of a smaller good, that he may avoid a greater injury, or expected jactura of a greater good; such is *fear of death*, of perpetual imprisonment, of chains, of stuprum, captivity, servitude, acrid tortures, infamy, disgrace, and losses of goods.

Fear of death is inflicted either by God or by men: by God, either intrinsically (as through perilous diseases), or extrinsically (as through shipwreck or something similar which has natural causes).

*A vow therefore made from such fear is valid*, as if one vowed something to God, if God should free him from a dangerous disease or from shipwreck.

From men fear is inflicted, either from *just cause* — as if one is thrown into chains for a crime, or condemned to death; or *through injury* — as if one threatens another with death unless he vows something.

Of a vow made from fear of the first genus, there is no doubt but that it is valid — one can vow something to God, if he shall free him from some ignominious jail or from the gallows, though owed to his sins.

Of a vow made from fear of the latter genus, some doubt, since it seems to be merely *involuntary*.

But it is not merely involuntary, but *mixed*, and pertains to the genus of actions which by *Aristotle* (*Ethic.* iii c. 1) are called *ἐκ μικτά* — not voluntary. For in this genus, insofar as the action itself we do not willingly, it is involuntary; but insofar as we choose less of two evils, it is voluntary. It is referred more to the actions of the will, by reason of the *principium eligens*.

Thus a vow which is extorted from someone under threat of death is *mixed of voluntary and involuntary*: for the vower is still master of his mind, and among two evils he chooses the less; therefore for coerced or involuntary it cannot be held. And on this account it must also be rendered — provided otherwise in the matter of the vow or in the vower himself there be no vice which can impede legitimate reasoning of the vow. *Exempli gratia*: someone under threat of death extorts a vow of paying a great sum of money into the use of the poor: here the extorter sins gravely, some coaction seems also to concur — but it is not simple coaction, since the vower has the power of choosing, and to redeem his life he chooses rather to lose a great force of his money from his goods; and the thing which is vowed is in itself honest and licit, that vow certainly cannot be made null.

*Aquinas* thinks indeed a vow of this kind, though valid by natural law, can be rescinded by the authority of the Church. But the Church does not have power of condensing laws obligating conscience, nor can establish anything against natural law, which agrees with divine law. Thus therefore it is to be established of vows which are made from fear of death.

*In general* about all perturbations of soul, know: insofar as they do not remove every judgment of reason, vows made from that perturbation still stand; but if they precede every judgment of reason, they become irritating — such were vows made from precipitate *iracundia*: for anger is short and brief and precipitates the mind, and takes away that true election which is required for legitimate vows. Therefore what is done in sudden motion of soul, is held for not done, unless afterwards the motion having been laid aside, by law it is repeated.

### Case X. Concerning a simulated vow — spoken publicly, but which he never meant to keep

**Respondeo.** A vow is *simulated* when something is promised to God with words that others may hear, but concerning which he never thought of performing. All simulation should be alien from our cult — *woe to those who serve God with a double heart* (Sir 1:28). And the Poet: *him I hate, whose thought is other than in his mouth, when other in his heart he conceals*.

If however one has made some vow simulate, he is bound to perform publicly what he said with his mouth — not indeed by *force of the vow itself* (for this ought to be done from full proposition of soul), but *by force of public right*, which ought to prevail.

If that vow, at first without proposition made, afterwards by tacit or express affect obtains acceptance, then also by force of the vow he is bound to perform. For what from the beginning was invalid, afterwards through supervening ratihabitio is confirmed — as is customary in other matrimonial promises.

### Case XI. Concerning vows made under condition

**Respondeo.** Most vows are made under condition. The condition, however, is either *honest and possible*, or *base and impossible*. A *base condition* makes the vow null — as if thou vow, if a thing is asked of thee, thou wilt do this or that.

If the condition is *naturally impossible*, or is falsified by lapse of time, such a vow made with that condition is null. *Exempli gratia*: if I vow, if some rich man donate to me many thousands of florins, then I will give alms, it is a vow with a naturally impossible condition, null. Or if I vow, if someone shall come to me after some months, whom I know already to have died long since, that I will do this or that. Such vows are foolish, and have no force.

If the condition is *base or turpe*, likewise a vow made under this condition is null. As if I vow, if thou hast committed homicide or theft, that I will do something in gratitude to thee: this vow does not oblige, since it moves another to sin.

A *possible and honest condition* is either *necessary* or *contingent*. A vow made under a necessary condition immediately obliges, since that condition is held as fulfilled — as if thou vow, when the sun arises tomorrow, that thou wilt return to labors: the vow is valid, to be fulfilled without delay.

A vow under a *contingent* condition is to be observed, when the condition is fulfilled — even when the condition is added with penal adjection, as if one vowed much money if he should incur some sin. For he who imposes the penalty would rather not have the condition fulfilled, lest he be compelled to sustain the penalty; but nevertheless he binds himself by vow to the penalty, if the vow of fleeing some sin he should not keep.

### Case XII. Whether a vow, which someone has vowed but cannot fulfill in person, can and ought to be discharged in his place by another

**Respondeo.** A legitimate vow, as hitherto described, must altogether be performed. So the Lord commanded (Deut 23:21–23): *when thou hast vowed a vow to the Lord thy God, thou shalt not slack to pay it — for the Lord thy God will surely require it of thee, and it would be sin in thee. But if thou shalt forbear to vow, it shall be no sin in thee. That which is gone out of thy lips, thou shalt keep and perform, according as thou hast vowed to the Lord thy God, of thy own proper will, and with thy mouth thou hast spoken*. A similar precept is repeated (Eccl 5:4; Sir 18:23). But often one is impeded by disease, captivity, war, or some other cause — even by death itself — so that he cannot in person perform what he vowed; in which case, what is to be done? For it is commonly said: *a vow belongs to the person, not to the thing* — whence no one seems bound by another's vow, so the guarantor is not held, unless he performs what another vowed.

*Sciendum igitur*: vows are of three genera: *real*, *personal*, or *mixed*. **Real** — another can perform in the place of another, as heirs for a defunct vower. *Exempli gratia*: if one vowed a certain sum of money into the use of the poor, for restoration of a chapel or something similar honest and possible, the heirs are bound by his vow to pay — not indeed as if they had vowed this, but because they undertake the estate of him who vowed.

**Personal** vows no one is bound to render for another, as if one vowed to fast, and prevented by death cannot perform, another in his name cannot legitimately fulfill.

**Mixed** vows in part are personal, in part real. These are to be rendered by others, especially heirs — not insofar as they are personal, but as they are real. As if one vowed a pilgrimage to a certain place, to bear a certain sum of money to poor exiles: the heir is not bound to the pilgrimage, but must give the money to the exiles. But if the vow was principally personal and from some part real, the heir, since he cannot be compelled to render the personal, is not bound even to the real, since with the principal taken away the accessory also is taken away — as if one vowed a pilgrimage to some place, the heir, when he is not obligated to the pilgrimage itself, is not even bound to the expenses which he could have made in doing the journey.

### Case XIII. Whether vows are to be made to merit something in the sight of God

**Respondeo.** In no way. All *merit* and all *perfection* of ours is to be ascribed to the most perfect Author, Christ; whatever of opinion is added to his mind, yields into sin. Not therefore does it lack impiety, that which is taught in the Papacy by *Aquinas* IIa IIae q. 88 art. 6, whom follows *Vasquez in Aquinas moral.* i, tit. 5 c. 3 *num. 5*: *by a vow a person becomes more noble, and merits therefore a greater reward from the same*.

Which is doubly false. **1.** Because all *merit* satisfying to God was performed *outside us*, and rewards indeed are given to those *acting well*, but not as of debt, but from a *free promise of God*, as elsewhere is taught. **2.** Because vows are *free* — nowhere does God exact them of us; but works are divinely commanded. Doubt however is none, but that to God more pleases what is done according to his command, than what is undertaken by our arbitrement.

Let no one therefore dare, by his vow, to attain merit — lest he render Christ's merit null. The end of our vows should be *the glory of God and the utility of the neighbor*. But by our own merits God is not honored, but rather ignominy is inflicted, as if he himself needs our merits, nor is the neighbor served, but our own commodity — retribution, namely, of the work — is sought.

### Case XIV. Concerning monastic vows — whether these too pertain to divine cult

**Respondeo.** Monasticism is the chief ornament of the Papacy, but happens to no one without vows. Vows pertain to the *substance* of the monastic order. Of these there are three, common to all monastic orders: **vow of voluntary poverty**, **vow of obedience to religious rule**, and **vow of perpetual continence** — the patronage of all which is undertaken by *Bellarmine*, lib. ii *De monach.* c. 30 *et sequentibus*.

The *Jesuit* sect retains indeed these three vows, but corrected and augmented — for it changes many in them and adds a fourth. For they vow *poverty* thus, that as long as they remain in *simple vow* (as they call it), they can acquire and possess the riches of the world in a straight and collateral line. The *vow of chastity* they temper thus, that one in simple vow, after ten years or thereabouts, can be absolved by the General of that order, and admitted to marriage. *Obedience* they vow to the Roman Pontiff and to the General of their order, far greater than that of monks of other orders — namely, that in all things (as the words of the vow hold, to which they may extend with charity and obedience), they should be as prompt as possible at his voice, so that they suffer themselves to be ruled by their superiors just as if they were a corpse — which anywise can be carried and treated in whatever way; or similarly, as an old man's staff, which wherever and in whatever way he who holds it in his hand wishes to use it, serves him: this they call *blind obedience*.

To all these they add a *fourth vow*, proper to the Jesuit order — which they call the *vow of mission*: by which they promise they shall undergo legations to whatever part of the earth they may be sent by the Roman Pontiff, for the commodity of the Roman See; which is not licit for monks of other orders. Of which see, among others, the *German Jesuit Catechism*, lib. ii c. 6 &c.

Although the Romanists attribute much to these vows, we hold nevertheless that they are illicit, and do not oblige in conscience — those act more rightly who altogether abstain from them than who perform them. Which we shall prove **1.** in general of all; **2.** in species of each.

**In general** let it be held: **(1)** that *these vows have not saints of men*, but of infidels and heretics as their examples — and for this cause alone should be alien from Christians. For *Jerome* lib. i *Contra Iovin.* c. 39, of the *Egyptian priests*, testifies: *when they had numbered the calves and cows and heifers, they never lived with women, until the number was completed*. Of the *Essenes* Josephus reports (lib. ii *Bell.* c. 7), and *Philo Iudaeus* (lib. de vita contempl.): *that they, casting off every solicitude of life, dwelt outside cities, and abandoned their own faculties, or made them common*; whence also *Polydor Vergil* (lib. 7 *De rer. invent.* c. 1) draws the origin of monks from the Essenes.

**(2)** Monastic vows are *directly against the ordination of God*. So *John of Daventria* wrote (*Harm. Confess. Aug.* p. 4): *in one and the same predicament are all these things: to place perfection in monasticism, and to hold that magistracy is not a state of perfection*. What is it to take away the ordination of God (as Paul calls magistracy, Rom 13), if this is not? They separate spouses, contrary to the precept of Christ (Matt 19:6): *what God has joined, let no man separate*; they seize a man outside the limits of his vocation, contrary to the precept of the Apostle Paul: *let each abide in the calling wherein he was called* (1 Cor 7:20); they remove offices due to parents (contrary to 1 Tim 5:8: *he that provideth not for his own, and especially his own domestic, hath denied the faith, and is worse than an infidel*); they call men away from labor to leisure, contrary to the precept of the Apostle: *he who works not, neither let him eat* (2 Thess 3:10).

**(3)** In these vows *manifest imposture* appears. For although at first sight they seem somewhat rigid, yet they admit an interpretation not ingrateful to the monks. For *poverty* they vow, not that they lack, but only that they abstain from possession of their own goods — otherwise, not destitute, they have need of the care of the body. *Obedience* they vow, not to God or parents (to whom they owed obedience by natural law), but to their prelates. *Continence* they vow, not from harlotry and libidos, but only from marriage: meanwhile to a votary who cannot contain, *concubines are permitted*, as *Wermerus* teaches (*Chirsat* c. 3 *De caelibatu sacerdot.*, prop. 6); and *Waldensis* (*Poggius Adopt. Papam*) affirms: *Pope Sixtus III can dispense, so that it is licit for a monk, casting off his cowl, to take a lawful wife, to serve as long as until the procreation of offspring; when however the offspring is produced, he returns to his cowl* (as *Hottomann* reports in *Bruto fulmine* p. 166, and *in iure canonico* 54, c. *Cum haberet* de restit.). *Panormitanus after morum uxoribus mariti ut episcopus consecratur — diaconus scilicet* (also in Panormitanus on Adopt. Papam, ambiguities).

**(4)** *The requisites of legitimate vows are not all found in monastic vows*. Vows ought to be from full proposition of soul: coerced or the vows of the immature are void. But in monastic vows, boys under puberty are admitted, and forced to it. So the Council of Carthage (can. 16) has: *it is decreed that lectors, when they come to the years of puberty, be compelled either to take wives or to profess continence*.

*Vows ought to be possible* and not to offer occasion of sinning. But *perpetual continence* is not possible for all who vow it — therefore from this vow have followed many illicit intercourses, stupra, infanticides, and more atrocious crimes; whence so many are the complaints of the ancients, that *the cloisters of nuns are brothels of harlots, and priests, under the pretext of handmaids, have concubines* (as *Gerson* deplores, *De defect. virorum ecclesiast.*). And *Hilarius Episcopus Augustinianus*, referring, whom *Chemnitz* part 1 *Examine* cites at year of Christ 860, in an epistle to *Nicholas Papam*, complains of Papal clerics: *that they yield to the wives of the fathers, the embraces of masculines and animals they do not abhor, in whatever most holy order they may be constituted, they do not doubt to abuse another's wives*, &c. All these things are from the impure vow of perpetual celibacy.

*Vows should not be meritorious*: but monastic vows have this chief end, that they should acquire *justice from sins* and merit *eternal life*. Therefore they call these vows *pair to Baptism* — that not only merit justice with God, but more: which as rightly said, *John of Daventria* commends (*Harmonia Confess.* Ang. p. 514). And *Bellarmine* writes (*De monach.* c. 6): *B. Anthony in vision heard holy angels say, all sins were remitted him, since he had undertaken a monastic life*. And *Thomas* teaches: *rightly it is believed, that through monastic profession one satisfies for all sins, and that profession is another baptism*. But in the confession of the Augsburg Confession (year 1530) instituted, it is expressly written that monastic life, kept with due observance, merits eternal life, and indeed Christ promises much greater to those who have left home, fields, &c. All which are *against the nature of legitimate vows* — therefore also from this head monastic vows are null.

*George Cassander*, a Papist writer (*in consult. ad Impp. Ferdinandum I et Maximilianum II*), art. 17, openly asserts and proves that vows are not meritorious of eternal life, when he thus writes: *in this kind of life (the monastic), if rightly it be observed according to the mind of the institutors, nothing shall we be able to find which we may reprehend in monastic life, but rather what we may admire and laud — only false opinion and preposterous confidence should be removed, lest confidence be placed in works, which should be placed in the sole merit of Christ*.

Which false opinion (says he) and preposterous confidence ought to be absent from every external cult and observance of ceremonies. What indeed shall we say — that both the more learned and more sane monks confess and profess this in public writings? For thus a certain *celebrated preacher of the Franciscans* in our age wrote as follows: *this*, he says, *is to be avoided, that justice or salvation be not placed in these observances, that is, that no one think he merits justice and remission of sins, and grace by observing these rites — but justification and grace are obtained by faith in Christ. Wherefore monks, according to their own rule, if for their own justice with God they proceed, err according to that of Christ (Matt 15): in vain do they worship me, teaching the mandates of men. Thus is to be judged of all traditions, ceremonies, and institutes, whatever they may be*.

Finally, it accedes, that *monastic vows are the more recent inventions of men*. For *John of Daventria* (*Harm. Confess. Aug.* p. 520) says: *whether in the time of Augustine the monastic vow was performed is uncertain — but certainly in the first Apostolic times they were still unknown, where those who had the gift of continence lived indeed celibate, but without vow or profession*. About the year of Christ 470 some began to profess *chastity totally* before the church, but even then no such vows were extorted anywhere. These therefore are the *general reasons*, which demonstrate the nullity of monastic vows.

**In species now of each**, easily can be shown how impious they are, contrary to the Word of God, and more pernicious to man — and therefore not to be admitted.

**As to the vow of poverty** — it fights with Scripture in various modes. For **(1)** God expressly prohibited, that there should be a *beggar* in his people (Deut 15:4); but the monks, to beg — by vow — bind themselves. And some of them teach the sanctity of themselves, that mendicancy is holy: contra Deut 15. And *in his own cult they contradict himself, if he claims his precepts in Ezek 14* (*erroneous*, sic).

**(2)** The whole *doctrine of the Gospel* is: not to *cast away* goods, but to *retain* them, that we may exercise beneficence toward the poor (1 Tim 6:18, Eph 4:28).

**(3)** The Apostle Paul does not will us to beg, but to give to the needy, and *therefore to labor with our hands, which is good* (Eph 4:28).

**(4)** *The vow of poverty*, by the example of Christ and the Apostles (whose imitators we ought to be), does not agree — since Christ did not vow money not collected from others, nor swear it off, but placed it in his purse (Matt 26:9; John 12:6); the Apostles bought necessary things with money (John 4:8); and Paul witnesses (Phil 4:12): *I know both to abound and to suffer penury*. Alms too he wills us to give thus, *not that ourselves be pressed to indigence* (2 Cor 8:13).

Furthermore, *the vow of poverty is contrary to the rule of the ancient monks*, who distributed their goods to the poor — but today's monks confer them to the monasteries, that as from a common fisc, without cares of the Word, they may live all the more broadly. But of old it was not so, as *Jerome* writes (*Ep. ad Heliodorum*): *whatever thou hadst of thine, and Cassian* (lib. iv *De institut. renunt.* c. 4): *he who has the smallest sum of money, sets not his soul in it, testifies*.

Moreover the vow of poverty is *mere illusion*. Either they are *poor* who make that vow — and thus stupidly they vow what they already are; or they are *rich*, and their goods are destined for monasteries, that from them they may live without solicitude: impious also, when they feign some merit from that vow.

Finally the vow of poverty is *in most, fallacious and dolous*. For they feign that they will be perpetually poor, but the *Benedictines* possess amplest funds, have the greatest vessels and sums of wealth. The *Franciscans* in England offered to the Roman Pontiff a great weight of gold and silver, that he might concede to them the faculty of possessing stable goods, as *Polydor Vergil* has in *Henry III*. The *Jesuits* reckon themselves in the order of mendicants — quite ridiculously, when they possess kingly wealth, and inhabit the palaces of princes; and now receive twenty times a hundred thousand gold coins each year from the properties of their colleges, as *Matthaeus Sethvitus* reports (*De monavistis Institut.* c. 22). *These are those poor, who while professing they have nothing, possess all things*, as *Crantius* writes (*Metropol.* lib. 5 c. 42).

The remaining monks fill their storerooms from missas mocked, from donations, confessions, and a thousand other arts — and yet all have vowed poverty; whence already in his time *Jerome* wrote (*Ad Nepotianum*): *monks are more richly than the seculars have been. They possess wealth under poor Christ, whom under rich devil they had not. The Church sighs at them who are rich, whom they used to hold as beggars once*.

**As to the vow of obedience** — it is contrary to Scripture, **(1)** because by this vow they make themselves slaves of abbots and prelates, and promise they shall live at their nod in matters of faith and conscience.
