But us the Apostle Paul otherwise instituted: *be not ye slaves of men*, he says, *because ye are bought with a price, ye are not your own but Christ's* (1 Cor 7:23).

**(2)** Christ says those sin, *who abandon the mandates of God on account of traditions of men* (Mark 7:8); but monastic obedience consists wholly in the mandates of men.

**(3)** No man can serve *two contrary masters*: this monks are forced to do, when by vow they obey abbots and prelates, often commanding contrary to God.

**(4)** Obedience especially of the *Jesuits* is a hiding-place of many crimes — they do not think they sin, when they lie before the king, and confirm a lie by oath: since they think when they lie, they have some command from their General, as is objected in the *Jesuit Catechism* (lib. ii c. 17). Under this impious vow of *singular devotion*, all their frauds, murders, and parricides — and yet this they sell under the name of singular devotion.

*See a specimen of Jesuit praxis, Edmund Campian in Epist. ad general. Societ. year 1585 Trevirii printed p. 27 and 33, who confesses, that in England he often was in the habit of changing his name — of Prayer's dementissim: also in Ep. de persecut. Ang. p. 126, l. 1, 514, mentitanoma p. 130, mentita veste p. 116* — names not rightly professed.

**As to the vow of perpetual continence** — it is contrary to Scripture. For **(1)** it calls continence *a singular gift of God*, which is *not granted to all* (Wis 8:21; 1 Cor 7:7; Matt 19:11). And yet the Romanists by this vow *without discrimination* bind all who profess monasticism and aspire to sacred orders, though they have not yet proved their strengths. But an infidel and foolish promise displeases God (Eccl 5:5); therefore he to whom the gift is not given, safer to abstain from this vow.

**(2)** Paul commands younger widows *to marry* (1 Tim 5:14). To such marriage is divinely enjoined as remedy of libido — against which precept virgins or widows expressly act, who vow perpetual continence.

**(3)** Paul says of him who cannot contain: *it is better to marry than to burn* (1 Cor 7:9). But the Romanists command those who feel passions to vow.

**(4)** Paul, when for certain causes he persuades virginity, wills not to inject a *snare on conscience* (1 Cor 7:35). But the Romanists inject the snare of virginity by vow, when they take from votaries all liberty of marriage.

**(5)** Paul writes that only he can remain in celibacy, *who has no necessity in his heart, but has power over his own will* (1 Cor 7:37). How therefore can they vow continence who have not firmness of heart, nor power of their own will — yet after profession is emitted, are forced to keep vows?

**(6)** The vow of continence is *diametrically opposed to the divine institution of marriage*, which he himself blessed (Gen 1) as the ordinary means of multiplying the human race. God gave no law of celibacy, but enjoined *matrimony on all who cannot contain, and are apt for it*.

**(7)** *Sterility was a disgraceful thing among the people of God* (Exod 23:26, Deut 7:14). But a vow of a disgraceful thing is rightly held for illicit.

**(8)** The vow of continence offers *occasion for vexation of body*: for many, that they be free from passions, macerate their bodies with fastings, whips, and similar disciplines — nor thus can they medicate their impurity. But *no man ever hated his own flesh, but nourishes and cherishes it, as Christ his Church* (Eph 5:29).

**From all these it is established, that monastic vows without wound of conscience cannot be well made.**

### Case XV. Whether monastic vows once made can be retained with a safe conscience, and whether those who have entered a monastery can return to the world and either contract marriage or seek some other honest office

**Respondeo.** Papists and *legislators of orders* who introduced them teach: *once vowed, always to be kept — for they are pledged with the whole church and sealed with commonplace precepts of God*. This is said to be the sentence of *Aquinas*, on the doctrine of *Iustus Lipsius Politicorum* lib. 2 c. 8, and of the celebrated distich of the *Charterhouse*: *what thou hast rashly vowed, and evilly promised, should be corrected — since what thou hast rashly promised, if not fulfilled, is more evil than what thou hast rashly vowed*.

But we hold quite the opposite. Monastic vows are not to be kept but to be rescinded — since they are illicit vows. All *illicit vows* are to be rescinded, since *what thou hast unwarily vowed, do not do* — as *Isidore* has, and *Gratian* after him (22 q. 4 c. 4). Since therefore in the preceding it has been sufficiently shown that monastic vows are illicit, therefore it follows they are to be rescinded. Nor is any *dispensation* required — the obligation of the vow is to be conquered, nor is any burden of conscience left upon the mind, since already in origin it was without foundation.

Which if it seem harsh to some, all who by such vows are entangled — to advance in obedience of God, to make honest life, to serve neighbor rightly, and to serve the family without disturbance of piety and without discrimination of sins and counsels — for those vows say they are *of counsel*, and pertain to the *state of perfection*: which if it is a null figment (since to divine precepts, which oblige all men, nothing can be more perfect), whence *Luther* in *De votis monasticis* rightly wrote: *those vows lean on the lies of impiety*.

Second: *whatever is not of faith is sin* (Rom 14). But monastic vows — perpetual, necessary, and not free — are without faith. For they lack justifying faith, since they cause a man to lean on his own merits. They lack faith of conscience, since no monk can be certain whether his monastic works are pleasing to God; nay this to assert they teach to be presumption; nay they always doubt whether they have satisfaction of works sufficient to merit. Which is *to serve God with doubting conscience*. Rightly therefore *Luther* in *De votis monasticis* with great confidence of spirit writes: *thus saith the Lord of hosts: monastic vows outside faith are sins made and kept, through this irritating, damnable, revocable and remissible, or otherwise to be vowed anew and kept*.

Third: monastic vows *fight with Christian liberty*, since they are perpetual and necessary, though impossible to us, to which consciences should not be obligated. For in every promise this condition is understood: *if I can*. *A monastic vow therefore*, says Luther (*De votis*, said opere), *ought necessarily to involve the liberty of remitting also, and to be interpreted in this sense: I vow to thee obedience, chastity, poverty to be kept as by the whole rule of Augustine unto death, freely, that is, that I may change when it seems fit. If thou interpret otherwise or hast understood, thou sinnest against the divine liberty given to us as mandate, nor can it be that God should otherwise accept, unless thou revoke thy liberty, that is, deny thyself*. More arguments, why here monastic vows are to be rescinded — since to wit they fight with the precepts of God, with faith, and with charity — see in *Luther* in that celebrated little book *De votis monasticis*.

Fourth: even the *Pope himself* teaches by his own example, that these vows can be relaxed, when he claims for himself the power of dispensing in vows, transferring monks from one order into another; nay he thinks he can abolish all orders of monks.

Fifth: nor are wanting *testimonies of the Fathers*, who after a proposition of continence judged marriage licit. *Augustine* calls the contrary opinion inconsiderate (*De bono viduitatis* c. 10): *those*, he says, *who by this inconsiderate opinion think, that if some of the female sex sworn to sanctity have married, they should not be marriages, do no small evil*. And in the 8th book he writes: *those who say the marriages of such (who namely have married having been loosed from their vow) are not marriages, but rather adulteries, do not seem sufficiently acute nor diligently to consider what they are saying*. In *Ep. 30 ad Bonifac.* he approves the marriages of such virgins. *Jerome* in *Ep. 8 ad Demetrianum* of virgins writes: *either they cannot contain themselves, or if they can, let them not judge others: but with divine authority*. Of votaries and nuns *Panormitanus* subscribes: *it is better that the fornicating virgin marry, than living openly as a harlot*. *Athanasius* to Draconium the monk permits the parents of children — how could this be done, if it were not licit for them to contract marriage? *Epiphanius* (*haer.* 61) writes: *better it is for one to sin openly through the exit itself, and take wife according to law, and to do a very long penance of virginity, than not to hurl the darts of Nicolaus into oneself*.

For these causes those merit praise, who by casting off the cowl show forth their monastic dress and give back to their conscience its liberty, to which the sins of the Romanists exposed themselves. Now they should not put back on the removed.

The examples finally come, of those who exited monasteries after the vow to the century: which *Brutchius*, from the Silesian noblest monastery of the Benedictine order, which is called *Cluztadia*, around the year of the Lord 724 — which to many of the children of Princes, Counts, and Nobles, who had been well instructed there, was free to leave, or take wives, or to enter courts of princes, or follow the militia. Around the year 637 *Dagobert*, king of the French, having laid aside the reign, undertook the monastic life; and *Boetius*, one deserting monastery, took up arms against *Pipin*, king, for occupying the reign. More examples see in *Ulspergensis De monachis* c. 28. Which, and similar things which no one ever culpated.

That, however, is objected to the place of the Apostle in 1 Tim 5, of widows *first faith made void* — nothing pertains against votaries or nuns; for in the time of Paul monastic vows were not used, but *of widows-almswomen* the Apostle speaks — who were said first to make faith irritating, since seduced from conscience and rejected from decorum, being wanton against Christ, they made shipwreck concerning faith; which evil that it might be forestalled, Paul selected only sexagenarians for that office. Nothing therefore about vows of nuns and often young girls, whose vow nowhere in Scripture is called faith: about which fully *Aegidius comment. cap. 5 epist. i ad Tim.* part 4 q. 5.

### Case XVI. Can parents rescind the vows of children?

**Respondeo.** Papists distinguish between those vows which pertain to *paternal right, power, and domestic government*, and those which do not incommode.

To the prior class they refer *vows of religion*, or monastic vows; to the latter, certain *political* — as abstinences, fasts, pilgrimages, &c. Whence some infer that parents, who tender the souls of adolescents and girls into monasteries with blandishments (as they themselves speak), in no way permit vows of children, though inconsiderate and to them impossible, to be rescinded.

And although the Canonists dispute, if the vow of religion is made by a son *before the fourteenth year*, or by a daughter *before the twelfth* — that it can be dissolved by the father (*Distinct.* 1 c. 1 *De regularib.* c. 6 *De religionibus*) — nevertheless not all admit this power of retracting to be reserved for parents, and only more expressly *Azorius* holds that no ancient authority has given parents power over children in vows, as one can see *Azorius* tom. 1 *Instit. moral.* lib. 11 c. 17 q. 9, nay even examples do not fail, that not even in this case are parents given power of rescinding vows of religion of impuberent sons or daughters: see the *Consilia Trainiensium* p. 3 con. 128 fol. 328.

But if in any thing parents have the right of retracting vows of children, certainly it is that on account of which they were feigned to be admitted to religion. **1.** Since already we have shown, in how many modes monastic vows are impious and illicit: from which anywise it is licit for children *by parents, at any time*, to be recalled.

**2.** The *rights of parents in children are perpetual*: nor by prescription of time, nor by any vow can they be removed. For the fourth precept is part of the moral law, which is eternal and immovable God's will: and it obliges children in all things which are not against the divine command. Therefore *Solomon* wills children to obey parents *in all things* (Prov 6:20).

**3.** From this power of parents it happens, that *God himself holds the vows of children as void, in which parents have not consented* (Num 30). Which is the exception of some vows — unless they are wholly moral, of offices of piety, where it is not presumed that Christian parents would dare to rescind them.

**4.** With divine law agree also *civil laws*, which sanction, that the vows of a *son under paternal power*, or of a family-son without consent of parents, do not stand (*l. fil. familias C. de fide-jussoribus*). Nor is a distinction to be made here between father and mother — since each parent is joined in the precept of *honoring parents*: as Tobit commends to his son especially the honor of his mother, who joined in service to him (Tobit 4:3). Therefore *Jodocus Lorinus* on the same place says: *not out of hatred but as a filial care demanded, the father should be honored, and after him the mother, whom one should venerate*.

**5.** Parents can *exact from children service which is due*. Paul wills children *to give recompense to their parents* (1 Tim 5:4); and *whoever has not care of his own, and especially of the domestic, has denied the faith, and is worse than an infidel* (v. 8). The offices of piety are impeded by monastic vows, in which children abjure parents, and *no longer honor* — nay they are compelled, as their own Chirmatius speaks, *to hate the parent by the vote of the Lord*.

**6.** With this Apostolic precept concords the *Council of Gangra*, which (c. 15) thus decrees: *if any of the children, giving pretense of religion, do not exhibit the due honor to their parents, let him be anathema*. Whence a fortiori it is inferred, that parents can revoke children whom they had bound by monastic vows.

**7.** If a wife cannot, against the will of her husband, and a servant cannot without consent of his master, enter a monastery — but can be called back by their superiors, which the Romanists themselves do not deny — why should not the same right belong to parents over children?

For although children after the years of puberty are no longer under paternal power, nevertheless such license is not conceded to them, that the *honor and obsequy* which is owed to parents by the fourth precept should be taken away: for always holds that of Prov 30:17: *the eye that mocks his father, the ravens shall pluck it out*.

These are the reasons which sufficiently show that parents can, over the *illicit vows* of children — especially monastic — *by best right retract*. What things *Bellarmine* (*De monach.* lib. 2 c. 36) with other Papists objects, we have elsewhere discussed. See *Chemnitz Examen* part 2 quaest. 15.

### Case XVII. Whether Papal dispensation from vows is possible

**Respondeo.** Much today is disputed among the Papists — where first are variously constituted the *modes of dispensing*, or of making vows irritating: **1.** through *absolution* (through which the vinculum of the vow is removed by means of penalties); **2.** through *commutation* into another good; **3.** through *relaxation*; **4.** through *redemption* — when the obligation of a pecuniary place of alms compensates.

Then they say the *power in vows* is not to be ecclesiastical or spiritual, but *civil or profane* — which competes to any superior, even a gentile, by reason of the thing or matter promised in the vow, when that is subject to his power, as far as it belongs to the superior.

**Third**, that *ecclesiastical superiors*, as bishops, abbots, and prelates, can dispense in the vows of clerics subject to them — but only insofar as they are subject to them in these things.

**Fourth**, that of the *Roman Pontiff*, they teach: he cannot dispense in the vows of laymen or seculars, since his care is not domestic but a stipulation of persons; but in the vows of clerics he can dispense in the whole church.

**Fifth**, if vows are of clerics who are free from every institute of religion, then they do not concede the Pope power of dispensing, unless he has vowed something which pertains to the government of churches, benefices, and ecclesiastical things — for then by tacit condition (namely if it should please the Roman Pontiff), the vow seems to have been made. If however the vows are of *monks and religious*, they say these can altogether be rendered irritating by the Pontiff, since they seem made under tacit condition, if it should please him. Of these things at length disputes *Casaeus, in his Instit. moral.* c. 17.

Others say the Pope alone can dispense over *five vows*: vow of perpetual continence, of religion, of pilgrimage to Jerusalem or Rome or to Compostella — unless the Pope has peculiarly conceded that power to some other person. In simple vow of perpetual chastity only the Pope can ordinarily dispense; but the bishop, when there imminent peril of evident incontinence, and the vower has some impediment of proceeding to the Pope. Thus is disputed by *Anthoninus* and other Papist authors (*Casus Larichim in Instit. moral.* lib. 4 c. 4).

*Silvester* also disputes (as I heard above), *in his 3 De adopt.*: *the Pope can dispense, so that it is licit for a monk, casting off his cowl for a time, to take a wife, namely as long as it takes for the procreation of offspring*.

Others dispute whether in dispensation or relaxation of a vow *just cause* is to be attended. Some deny — that indeed the Pope over a vow without rational cause can dispense sufficiently. And for *rational causes* they hold: **1.** difficulty or precipitation in vowing; **2.** weakness of the one bearing the vow; **3.** necessity or utility, either public — great necessity, great utility private; **4.** every event, from which the fulfillment of the vow becomes useless or noxious, or impediment of a greater good; **5.** every other cause, which the superior has judged sufficient for adding.

*Others hold*: to rescind a vow *just cause is not needed*, but the mere will of him who has power over it suffices. And this opinion *Silvester* names truer (*i q. 3*), since vows are made under tacit condition — *if they should please the superior*.

Then they dispute, whether one can *privately commute one vow into another*, and most conclude that he can, when it is changed into an evidently better good — as *Aquinas* says: *no proposition, or thing promised, obliges, when it is changed into a better* (as Gregory said). But when it is doubtful whether some good is better, then the authority of the superior is needed.

And indeed penes the Pope they say is *the power of commuting* all vows of Christians wherever they live; but under bishops is the power, of commuting at least those, whose commutation is not reserved to the Roman Pontiff. But they say the power of commuting is held by him who has power of dispensing. Then commutation is made: **1.** when the thing which is vowed was useless; **2.** when difficulty arises in performing; **3.** the vow is made from sudden consternation. And this commutation is made with consideration of the qualities of the person. For one about to commute a vow of pilgrimage into a divine service, or of poor labor, indeed he will change certain days into fastings and prayers, and the expenses which he would have made, besides those he would have made at home, into alms — so that on the rich he imposes greater burdens than on the poor. If however anyone is impeded in the fulfillment of a vow at some certain time, then they say it suffices, if the commuter does not remove the vow itself, but grants only a delay of time.

These disputations of the Papists flow from that *spurious empire* which they attribute to prelates, especially to the Pope, over the consciences of men — which is tyrannical and altogether contrary to the Word of God, which recognizes only God as *Lord of consciences*.

No exemplum of such dispensation or rescission exists in the *sacred Bible*. What if the same Pope, thinking he can rescind a vow without any cause, when only his will suffices? Since God, not to the will of the Pope or to the reasoning of the church rules his Church, it is rash to expose consciences to the will of one man without just cause.

Therefore it is to be experienced, how consciences are burdened, when the Pope gives power to monks of transferring from one order into another — which is nothing else than to rescind the vow of the prior order (by which nevertheless they thought consciences bound), and to impose a new yoke by a new vow. From which entanglements the wretched cannot emerge before recognizing that Papal power of imposing and rescinding vows to be null.

But rather: **1.** if the *matter of the vow* was illicit, the vow is by that very fact null, and needs no peculiar dispensation.

**2.** If the vow was *licit and legitimately made*, then it admits no dispensation. For God's mandate of *rendering* vows freely and licitly made is clear (Deut 23:22, Ps 50:14, Eccl 5:5).

Impious therefore is the voice of *Lorichius* (*Institut. cathol.* c. 15): *some impediment supervening to the fulfillment of a vow, the Pope declares that the vow does not oblige: therefore he does not remove divine right, but only explicates*.

But it is licit to no man to dispense against divine right, and where God has not added explication, at our arbitrament divine precepts are not to be explicated. No impediment can there be, which either can remove our obedience to divine mandates, or can suspend it.

The vow of perpetual continence, of religion, of pilgrimage to Jerusalem &c. to fulfill — is not of divine right; therefore it does not need dispensation, but by that very fact is null and illicit, on the causes above indicated.

*So much of vows.*
